Temples, C. Cap, C. L. Chang, Y. -Y. Chang, M. Garcia-Sciveres, W. Ho, K. Li, X. Li, R. Linehan, M. Lisovenko, K. Okubo,  Manabputra, K. Ramanathan, S. Ray, B. Sandoval, A. Suzuki, G. Wang, M. Williams, J. R. Xiong, V. Yefremenko, B. L. T. Plourde, S. R. Golwala, K. Stifter, N. A. Kurinsky
Abstract: We report on the position- and temperature-dependent response of a kinetic inductance phonon-mediated (KIPM) detector. To study position-dependence, we used a controllable mirror attached to a micro-electromechanical system (MEMS) to steer optical light with a 100 $\mu$m spot size over the backside of the cryogenic detector. With both pulsed and continuous operation of the LED, we observe features of the detector housing and find that the signal is strongest when the LED is pointed directly at the KID. The "off-KID" response is measured to be 34% of the "on-KID" response and is largely independent of direction. We model phonon propagation and collection in the KIPM detector with G4CMP and assert that the isotropic angular dependence is unlikely to be due to discrete phonon absorption "sinks", such as the microwave feedline or the device mounting points, but can be explained instead by an isotropic energy loss in the crystal. We furthermore utilize the temperature flexibility of the MEMS and KIPM detector system to investigate temperature-dependent responsivity, both at the sensor level (i.e., frequency shifts in the KID) and at the detector level (i.e., position dependence). We fit the KID's response to a two-component heat capacity and present the model as an alternative way to understand the KID's response to absorbed energy. We show on- and off-KID data at a range of temperatures and show that the phonon dynamics are not temperature dependent. We comment on the resulting implication for KID dynamics and design improvements.

### Paper [206]
Title: Distilling Large-Scale Machine Learning Interatomic Potentials to Predict Long-Timescale Electrolyte Transport Properties
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39513
Authors: Ishan Amin, Yue Jian, Nitesh Kumar, Christine Zhang, Jianwei Lai, Casey S. Mezerkor, Aaron M. Melemed, Jonathan R. Thurston, Neil P. Dasgupta, J. David Bazak, Ethan J. Crumlin, Samuel Blau, Aditi S. Krishnapriyan
Abstract: Accurately predicting the transport properties of liquid electrolytes is critical for next-generation battery design and requires force fields that are both quantum-mechanically accurate and fast enough for long-timescale simulation. Recent large-scale pretrained machine learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) approach density-functional-theory accuracy, but remain too slow to access the necessary timescales to converge transport properties. We provide a knowledge distillation procedure to transfer the accuracy of a large-scale "teacher" MLIP into fast, system-specific "students", without any additional ab initio data. Our distilled models run ~40x faster, reproduce teacher-predicted structural observables, and produce stable 20 ns trajectories in days, rather than the months the teacher would require. Across a diverse set of sodium- and lithium-ion electrolytes, student-predicted self-diffusivities, ionic conductivities, and shear viscosities track experiment and outperform classical force-field predictions, establishing distillation as a practical route to accurate, long-timescale simulations necessary for high-throughput electrolyte screening.

### Paper [207]
Title: Computing with Coupled Oscillator Networks: Optimization, Memory, and Modern AI Primitives
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39703
Authors: Ahmed Allibhoy, Fabio Pasqualetti
Abstract: We explore how synchronization in coupled oscillator networks may be employed to perform a variety of computing tasks. These systems are becoming more attractive as an alternative to conventional digital computers due to the increasing energy demands of the latter, particularly for large-scale problems in machine learning and artificial intelligence. The ubiquity of coupled oscillator networks means that oscillator-based computing can be thought of as a universal computing paradigm encompassing systems with a diversity of physical realizations, e.g. as analog circuits, neurological systems, spintronic systems. The bridge between the dynamics of coupled oscillator networks and computational primitives they implement is the Ising model, which originates in statistical physics, and when applied to oscillator networks, explains the emergence of globally complex and disordered synchronization patterns from local interactions in the network. We discuss the application of coupled oscillator networks to three fundamental computing operations. First, oscillator networks as specialized hardware solvers of NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems, with the solution encoded in the synchrony patterns. Second, oscillator networks as associative memory systems, enabling storage and retrieval of data represented through phases of oscillations. Finally, oscillator networks as a dynamical implementation of attention, with synchronization providing the mechanism for the alignment of keys to queries. The ability of oscillator networks to implement these computational primitives paves the way for novel realizations of intelligent systems through a variety of physical substrates.

### Paper [208]
Title: Non-Markovian quantum-emitter dynamics and bound-state formation in an Aubry--Andr\'{e}--Harper photonic lattice
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39707
Authors: Zahra Jalali-Mola, Jule Neidhart, Saeid Asgarnezhad-Zorgabad, Ortwin Hess
Abstract: In non-Markovian light-matter interactions, a spectrally structured bath can alter the evolution of a quantum emitter by modifying the vacuum fluctuations experienced by the emitter. This raises the question of how the localization transition and the associated multifractal critical modes induced by a quasiperiodic potential shape the emitter dynamics. We investigate this scenario in the single-excitation sector for a two-level quantum emitter coupled to an Aubry-Andr\'e-Harper (AAH) photonic lattice. By externally tuning the strength of the quasiperiodic onsite modulation, the lattice is tuned from the extended to the localized regime through a critical point characterized by multifractal modes. Using modal analysis, we show that the quasiperiodic modulation reshapes the local density of states at the coupling site and thereby controls the effective spectral coupling between the emitter and the lattice. The transient dynamics depend strongly on the spatial and spectral overlap between the emitter and the lattice modes: extended modes below the critical modulation generally produce rapid depopulation, whereas critical and localized modes give rise to coherent back-action and persistent Rabi-like oscillations, with particularly pronounced behavior at the critical point. We further identify emitter-photon bound states whose formation is controlled by the quasiperiodic spectral structure of the bath and whose overlap with the initially excited state leads to partial long-time population trapping. These results establish a direct connection between quasiperiodic localization, non-Markovian dynamics, and emitter-photon bound-state formation.

### Paper [209]
Title: Maximum-Entropy Ensemble of Quantum Subsystems with a Fixed Average State
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39712
Authors: Charlie Shakeshaft, Christopher David White, Harry J. D. Miller
Abstract: The reduced density matrices of Haar-random bipartite states form a fixed-trace version of the Wishart ensemble of random matrices, with an asymptotic eigenvalue density given by the Marchenko--Pastur (MP) law. We establish a similar universal form for the maximum-entropy distribution of mixed states subject to a fixed ensemble average, arguing that its eigenvalue density converges asymptotically to that of the correlated Wishart ensemble (CWE). The density is thus obtained by a free multiplicative convolution formula, which we apply to the transverse-field Ising model (TFIM) with a thermal average state to derive a high-temperature leading-order correction to the MP law. We then consider measurement by a random POVM on the purification of the mixed reference state. We show that the resulting mixed-state ensemble is asymptotically equivalent to the CWE, and provide confirming numerical evidence. We return to the TFIM example to compute the entanglement of the system with the ancilla after measurement, and give finite-temperature Page curves for the average entanglement entropies.

### Paper [210]
Title: Going deep, reaching further: What can Boltzmann machines efficiently represent?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39731
Authors: Tobias Reinhart, Gemma De les Coves
Abstract: While universal approximation theorems theoretically underpin the representation capabilities of machine learning models, they have two shortcomings: They do not focus on efficient representations and they tend to be insensitive to depth and other heuristically important properties. In this work we show that these two shortcomings are intertwined. We prove that machine learning models which are deeper or have higher-order interactions can efficiently represent a larger set of functions, i.e. have larger efficient reach. It follows that deep Boltzmann machines (DBMs) have maximal efficient reach, restricted Boltzmann machines (RBMs) sub-maximal, and k-body generalizations of RBMs form a hierarchy between the two, even though all are universal. More generally, (i) we provide a full characterization of machine learning models with maximal efficient reach; (ii) we derive separations between deep and shallow models for three representation tasks; and (iii) we prove that the efficient reach of shallow models constitutes an infinite hierarchy indexed by the interaction order. The proofs draw on different methodologies, namely on (i) a recent characterization of universal spin models; (ii) computational complexity bounds for spin models; and (iii) communication complexity bounds applied to shallow models. This work shows that universal machine learning models can strongly differ in their efficient representation capabilities, and provides a theoretical foundation for heuristic observations.

### Paper [211]
Title: Ghosts in the nanomachine: dynamical slowing-down in the phase-space of nanomechanical strings
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39968
Authors: Samer Houri, Agnes Zinth, Menno Poot
Abstract: The linear behavior of micro- and nanomechanical devices has been the topic of intense study over the past years. Their nonlinear behavior, on the other hand, continues to provide new and interesting dynamics. This work leverages a cavity opto-mechanical system whose very high sensitivity and low dissipation rate allows to capture the dynamics of fast transients that occur when strongly-driven nonlinear resonators relax to their equilibrium state. We experimentally study the ringdown dynamics of a nanomechanical Duffing resonator in both the time domain and in phase space. The resonator exhibits very different ringdown dynamics depending on whether it is subject to one or two tone driving. In the former case the ringdown follows a regular exponential decay, whereas the latter case shows exotic dynamics such as libration, slowing-down while scattering in phase-space, and ghost slowing-down. We demonstrate that this distinct difference can be attributed to the structure of the phase space itself. Surprisingly, we find that the energy in the resonator changes non-monotonously and that long delay between turning off the drive and the decay of the motion amplitude results in dynamical meta-states.

### Paper [212]
Title: A physics-informed uncertainty-aware surrogate for $p$-$n$ junction diodes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39993
Authors: Suchita Kulkarni
Abstract: We develop an uncertainty-aware physics-informed surrogate for a one-dimensional $p$-$n$ junction diode, conditioned on device length, doping and bias. Using the internal state of the device as predicted by the surrogate we develop a test-suite of quantities of interest including the terminal current, depletion width, peak field and stored charge. The formalism and the quantities are can be extended to asymmetric junction and two-dimensional diodes as shown by means of benchmark analysis. For the one-dimensional symmetric junction surrogate, we obtain 0.11% median $L_1$ error on the electrostatic potential, and 1.5% and 0.61% on the electron and hole densities. Finally, we develop an uncertainty analysis which determines the validity of surrogate answer within the user-specified tolerance.

### Paper [213]
Title: Electrochemical functionalization of graphene rationalized by Hammett and Swain-Lupton parameters
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39997
Authors: L. Chen, H. Li, C. Li, X. Zhang, Q. Dai, M. Alhashmi, J. Tong, E. Hoenig, Y. Li, A. Campbell, K. Hu, Y. -C. Soong, C. Sevik, L. F. Vega, F. M. Peeters, M. Lozada-Hidalgo
Abstract: Graphene's sp^{2}-bonded carbon lattice makes it exceptionally resistant to covalent functionalization, with existing approaches relying on chemically specific and often harsh methods, fragmenting our understanding of its reactivity. Here we show that electric fields of approximately 1 V nm^{-1} in non-aqueous electrolytes enable the electrochemical functionalization of monolayer graphene with a broad range of substituents, leading to two-order-of-magnitude conductance modulation stable over 5,000 cycles and semi-reversible electronic conductor-insulator transitions. Analysis of the oxidation potentials across substituents reveals trends governed by classical descriptors. Species forming localised {\sigma} bonds display increasing oxidation potentials with the meta-Hammett constant ({\sigma}_m), whereas substituents interacting via delocalised {\pi} orbitals follow an inverse dependence on the modified Swain-Lupton resonance parameter (R). These relationships indicate that graphene's oxidation potential increases with the electron-withdrawing strength of the substituent. Our results provide new insights into the electrochemical activity of inert materials under strong electric fields.

### Paper [214]
Title: Dissipative controller as a hidden source of errors in quantum gates
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40072
Authors: Serhat C. Kad{\i}o\u{g}lu, Saulo V. Moreira, Mark T. Mitchison, Anthony Kiely
Abstract: Quantum systems are typically controlled by classical devices that necessarily dissipate energy to the surrounding environment. By the fluctuation-dissipation theorem, this implies noise in the control field produced. We investigate the relationship between the resulting errors and the entropy produced by the macroscopic controller, working in the context of a concrete minimal model. In the most experimentally relevant regime of fast control and high fidelity, we find a tradeoff between dissipation and control errors. These findings are exemplified by perturbative calculations of the gate fidelity for simple gate implementations and of the fidelity of state transfer via shortcuts to adiabaticity in the Landau-Zener model. Our results quantitatively demonstrate that suppressing errors in quantum control typically comes at the cost of macroscopic dissipation, which is needed to tame fluctuations in the classical controller.

### Paper [215]
Title: The coupled spread of information and potential-driven motion in evacuating agent populations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40081
Authors: Benjamin A. Jasperson, Logan Dehay, Rahul Gulati, Siddhartha Srivastava, Krishna Garikipati
Abstract: Whether a population evacuates safely depends on two coupled processes: how a warning spreads through it, and how that information moves people. We model this coupling with an agent-based framework, in which each agent's information state--susceptible, exposed, informed or reverted--gates its motion. Only informed agents respond to a global background potential encoding hazards and safe zones, while all agents interact locally through a Lennard-Jones potential. The total potential experienced by an agent drives an Ito stochastic differential equation. Tests show that landscapes with potential minima leave far fewer agents never exposed than in a flat, uniform potential control landscape, as informed agents driven toward the minima sweep past susceptible neighbors. In our tests, conduit routes raise the fraction of agents in the safe-zone minima, but leave greater numbers of agents never exposed, when compared to an unguided evacuation. This is attributed to conduits shortening trips at the cost of informed agents having fewer encounters with susceptible neighbors who could receive their information. Independently, the effective information transmission rate estimated from these encounters falls roughly sixty-fold below its well-mixed value within the first time unit, because state transitions create spatial mechanisms of net transmission suppression that mean-field closure does not capture.

### Paper [216]
Title: Multi-excited random walks on general trees
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40084
Authors: Jack Mills, Tuan-Minh Nguyen
Abstract: We study multi-excited random walks on infinite locally finite trees. The model is described by placing a stack of ``cookies'' at each vertex, with one cookie consumed on each successive visit. As long as cookies remain, the walk is in an excited regime and makes nearest-neighbour steps according to a prescribed sequence of biases. Once the stack is exhausted, the walk is in a non-excited regime and moves uniformly among the neighbouring vertices. The model is closely connected to foraging ecology, where repeated visits to a location can deplete local resources and thereby influence the attractiveness of that location for future visits. We prove that the process exhibits a sharp phase transition between recurrence and transience. The critical threshold is determined by the branching-ruin number of the tree, which is equal to the Hausdorff dimension of the tree's boundary under a suitable metric. Our result substantially extends Zerner's recurrence-transience criterion for multi-excited random walks on $\mathbb Z$ [Probab. Theory Related Fields 133 (2005)] to general trees.

### Paper [217]
Title: Multistable nematic liquid crystal pores as reconfigurable optical vortex metasurfaces
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40123
Authors: Claire Dor\'e, Brian Edwards, Nader Engheta, Kathleen J. Stebe
Abstract: Nematic liquid crystals confined in cylindrical pores adopt multiple stable director topologies --- radial, escaped, and arches --- each imprinting a distinct optical signature on transmitted circularly polarized light, with topological charge set by the projected director winding number, $\ell = 2m$. Using Landau--de Gennes and Jones-calculus modeling validated against micron-scale experiments, we show that this mechanism persists as the pore diameter shrinks toward the optical wavelength. However, full-wave Maxwell simulations of periodic sub-wavelength pore arrays reveal that the resulting vortex is a near-field phenomenon only: each topology imprints a distinguishable phase-singular signature --- differing in core multiplicity, core width, and ring amplitude --- directly below the pore array, but this signature does not survive propagation into the far field, where diffraction and inter-pore interference erase it within about one wavelength. Landau--de Gennes simulations further demonstrate that an applied electric field switches the pore between topologies, and hence between their near-field optical signatures. Because each topology is locally stable, sub-wavelength NLC pore arrays constitute an electrically reconfigurable, non-volatile platform for topology-encoded near-field optical readout, with potential application to optical labeling and anti-counterfeiting.

### Paper [218]
Title: Scale-free wave localization at topological multicriticality
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40125
Authors: Zhijie Xue, Yuan Tian, Nanyu Chen, Xiujuan Zhang, Tao Li, Shuang Zhang, Fuxin Guan
Abstract: Spatial wave localization at boundaries is conventionally restricted to exponential decay. Here, we observe a scale-free boundary mode exhibiting anomalous polynomial decay, highlighted by strictly linear spatial envelopes, driven by topological multicriticality in acoustic metamaterials. We establish a universal geometric bulk-boundary correspondence rooted in singularity theory by mapping n-th order topological multicriticality onto Arnold An catastrophe. We prove that defectiveness in the spatial transfer matrix enforces a (n-1)-th power spatial decay of boundary modes. At a topological tri-critical point, the singularity of winding function manifests as an A2 fold catastrophe. This dictates a strictly linear spatial decay, marking a sharp departure from the conventional exponential scaling paradigm. Using acoustic metamaterial platforms tuned to a tricritical point, we experimentally demonstrate this linear decay envelope in 1D, and further generalize it to two-dimensional systems to realize scale-free corner modes with simultaneous linear decay along orthogonal directions. While existing critical topology frameworks rely on entanglement spectra to diagnose mode existence in gapless regimes, our work bridges catastrophe theory with topological matter at multicriticalities, advancing the field from abstract mode identification to deterministic real-space wavefunction engineering.

### Paper [219]
Title: Does System Size Matter in Uncertainty Quantification for Machine Learning Interatomic Potentials?
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40126
Authors: Chayaphol Lortaraprasert, Nontawat Charoenphakdee, Wenwen Li, Yong-Bin Zhuang, Yuta Tsuboi
Abstract: Machine-learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) commonly use uncertainty estimates to flag out-of-distribution (OOD) configurations and select structures for active learning. A key question is whether the resulting structure-level uncertainty scores remain comparable across different system sizes. A common practice is to aggregate per-atom uncertainties by their mean or maximum and compare the resulting score against a fixed threshold, implicitly assuming such comparability. We test this assumption across several in-distribution (ID)/OOD pairs by characterizing both aggregations as a function of atom count. The two aggregations exhibit distinct size dependencies. Mean aggregation remains size-independent for homogeneous systems but loses sensitivity to defects in large cells. In contrast, maximum aggregation grows with system size for ID systems while remaining approximately flat for defect-containing systems, narrowing the ID-OOD gap in a manner consistent with extreme-value statistics. Although highly uncertain atoms in large structures may indicate novel local environments, retraining on these structures yields little improvement, in contrast to retraining on OOD structures constructed to lie outside the training distribution. Our results reveal a practical limitation of size-unaware uncertainty criteria and motivate size-aware calibration for more reliable OOD detection in large-scale atomistic simulations.

### Paper [220]
Title: Three-dimensional QCA that are not stably circuits
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40235
Authors: Bowen Yang
Abstract: We prove that the three-dimensional chiral $\Z_p$ Clifford quantum cellular automata of Haah and of Sun--Yang--Wang--Tantivasadakarn--Chen are, for every odd prime $p$, not finite-depth quantum circuits, even after tensoring with arbitrary ancillary degrees of freedom and composing with translations. Previously such statements were known only inside the Clifford category. As a consequence, the group of three-dimensional QCA modulo stabilized circuits and translations is not finitely generated. The key ingredient is a new invariant of invertible subalgebras, which we conjecture to be the square of the total quantum dimension of the hosted anyon theory, modulo rational squares.

### Paper [221]
Title: Bulk gap stability and spectral flow for fermions with weak long-range interactions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40299
Authors: Stefan Teufel
Abstract: We prove that weak long-range many-body interactions preserve the bulk gap of a free fermion system and that a quasi-local spectral flow transports the free ground state to ground states of the interacting system. Let $\Phi_h$ be a free fermionic interaction, possibly including pairing terms, whose infinite-volume Majorana coefficient operator $h$ has no spectrum in $(-g,g)$, and let $\mathcal V$ be an even self-adjoint many-body interaction. For sufficiently small $|\lambda|$, we construct pure ground states $\omega_{\lambda}$ of the interacting bulk system $\Phi_{\lambda}=\Phi_h+\lambda\mathcal V$ and quasi-local automorphisms $\beta_{0,\lambda}$ such that $\omega_{\lambda}=\omega_0\circ\beta_{0,\lambda}$, where $\omega_0$ is the free ground state of $\Phi_h$. Each $\omega_\lambda$ has a GNS Hamiltonian with gap at least $g-C_*|\lambda| \|\mathcal V\|_*$, where $\|\cdot\|_*$ is the relevant interaction norm.
  Our result allows polynomial decay of both the free coefficients and the many-body interaction. For two-site interactions on a uniformly discrete point set $\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^d$, a sufficient condition is decay as $(1+|x-y|)^{-\varpi}$ with $\varpi>2d$, for both the hopping and pairing coefficients and the interaction terms. The spectral-flow generator preserves the spatial exponent of the polynomial interaction norm, and its automorphisms satisfy locality bounds at that exponent. If $h$ and $\mathcal V$ have stretched-exponential decay, so does the spectral-flow generator.
  The proof constructs the spectral flow and establishes the gap through an excitation-number comparison, using polynomial propagation bounds for evolved interactions proved in a companion paper.

### Paper [222]
Title: Local determination of low-energy states in gapped fermion systems with weak long-range interactions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40337
Authors: Stefan Teufel
Abstract: We consider bulk-gapped fermion systems whose infinite-volume bulk is obtained by adding sufficiently weak even many-body interactions to a free Hamiltonian with spectral gap $g>0$, allowing polynomial spatial decay of both parts. We prove quantitative local determination of low-energy states: whenever another interaction agrees with the bulk interaction inside a finite or infinite region $U$, with bulk terms crossing its boundary unchanged or removed, all its states with excitation energies in $[0,3g/4]$ have local expectations close to those of the bulk ground state deep inside $U$. The error decays with the distance to the complement of $U$, independently of the interaction terms supported outside $U$ and of the chosen low-energy state.
  Quantitative local determination has strong consequences. (i) The bulk interaction has a unique algebraic ground state. (ii) A strong "local perturbations perturb locally" (LPPL) principle holds: arbitrary distant defects change local ground-state expectations only by a spatially decaying error. (iii) Low-energy excitations in finite samples remain localized near the boundary for all times, up to spatially decaying tails, uniformly over arbitrary even boundary terms. (iv) Correlations from deep inside $U$ to outside $U$ are small, with quantitative bounds in terms of spatial separation and distance to $U^c$.
  To prove local determination of low-energy states, we combine quasi-adiabatic transport of the free ground state to the interacting bulk ground state with a new condition, local excitation detectability (LED). A companion paper establishes gap stability and constructs the transport for weak interactions, while we prove LED at sufficiently weak coupling. The abstract theorem is nonperturbative and applies whenever the transport and LED hypotheses hold.

### Paper [223]
Title: Exact holographic superfluid at the Bogomolny point: transport, large charge, and R\'enyi entropies
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40371
Authors: Siqing Yu
Abstract: The Abelian Higgs model in AdS$_5$, in the probe limit, is a standard holographic model of a strongly coupled superfluid, usually solved numerically. When the scalar mass saturates the Breitenlohner-Freedman bound and the quartic coupling takes the Bogomolny value, its condensed phase can be found in closed form on every static, uncharged AdS$_5$ background with maximally symmetric spatial sections. We prove that it is linearly stable, as is any nodeless probe-limit condensate in the standard quantization with a nonnegative quartic coupling and no superflow. On each slicing it answers a different question about the dual conformal field theory. On the planar black brane it is the superfluid at all temperatures below $T_c$: the superfluid fraction follows the Gorter-Casimir two-fluid law exactly, and the speed and the attenuation of sound are found in closed form. In global AdS$_5$ it describes a state of charge $Q$ on $\mathbb{R}\times S^3$, arguably the lowest-energy one, and gives its scaling dimension in closed form at all $Q$, classically in the bulk. Its excitations include towers that the superfluid effective theory does not describe; they come from an infrared fixed point of the probe sector. The hyperbolic slicing gives in closed form a homogeneous hairy phase of the black holes that compute the charged R\'enyi entropies of a ball. Its condensate does not vanish at the entangling surface. When sourced by operators inserted there, the phase sets in at R\'enyi index exactly one at zero chemical potential, as found for a neutral scalar.

### Paper [224]
Title: Framework-Conditional Open Materials Generation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40443
Authors: Maya M. Martirossyan, Hillary Pan, Philipp H\"ollmer, Stefano Martiniani
Abstract: Theoretical predictions of novel materials rely on a variety of techniques, from data-mining-based ion substitution methods to fully de novo generative models. Each approach imposes specific constraints and encounters unique challenges when extrapolating beyond the distribution of known materials to discover new ones. The foremost goal of materials generation is to produce stable, unique, and novel (S.U.N.) structures. Predicted structures that meet these criteria, however, typically result from novel chemical compositions mapped onto known structural prototypes or frameworks. We leverage this heuristic insight to present a new generative paradigm for the de novo prediction of inorganic crystalline materials constrained by their structural framework---i.e., the unit cell shapes and respective particle positions. Our method, termed OMatG-FC (Open Materials Framework-Conditional Generation), learns via a combination of discrete flow matching on the atomic species and stochastic interpolants for the volume scaling of the unit cell lattice, while fixing in-place the fractional coordinates and the unit-volume lattice vectors, dramatically reducing the dimensionality of the problem compared to unconstrained (framework-free) generation. Our method is more flexible than existing prototype-constrained methods, demonstrating capability for discovery of unseen, stable prototypes derived from known structural frameworks. We also investigate the number of unique frameworks in the MP-20 dataset, demonstrating the propensity of duplicate frameworks within the dataset and providing a framework-split version. We benchmark the performance of our model using the LeMat-GenBench suite using three ML interatomic potentials and achieve state-of-the-art S.U.N. rate below the convex hull, eschewing the need to learn atomic packings for materials discovery.

### Paper [225]
Title: ML-COHP: predicting bond descriptors using latent space of a universal interatomic potentials
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40445
Authors: Daniil S. Alekseev, Ilya V. Chepkasov, Alexander G. Kvashnin
Abstract: The integrated crystal orbital Hamilton population (ICOHP) is the standard measure of bond strength in a DFT calculation, but every structure needs its own DFT single point and LOBSTER projection, which takes days for a cell of a hundred atoms, such as a grain boundary or a catalytic nanoparticle. We predict ICOHP from the frozen descriptors of a MACE foundation model. A symmetric pair head reads the scalar and bond-projected vector channels of the two atoms and corrects a pair baseline built from the element embeddings of the same model. We tested the model on the high-entropy diboride (Hf,Zr,Ti,Nb,Ta)B$_2$, Pt clusters on defective graphene and Pt/Pd nanoparticles with adsorbed O$_2$. The error on bonds stronger than 0.5 eV is 1.6-4.1%, about half that of the Gaussian-process model of Ferreira et al. trained on the same data. Trained only on high-temperature HEB$_2$ snapshots, the model reproduces the LOBSTER ICOHP of relaxed grain boundary cells to 0.06 eV in about a second, against 5-51 hours of LOBSTER. The vector channels halve the error compared to the scalar ones, and the baseline keeps the model usable with two training structures. With geometries from a fine-tuned MACE potential, the model gives the bonding of Pt/Pd particles over a continuous O$_2$ coverage, and DFT on these particles shows that O$_2$ costs a Pt-rich shell several times more metal--metal bonding than a Pd shell.

### Paper [226]
Title: Nonlinear Dynamical Systems as Scalable and Efficient Physical Neural Networks
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40471
Authors: Tyler King, Magnus Saebo, Jeremie Laydevant, Haoting Zhen, Maxwell G. Anderson, Tatsuhiro Onodera, Peter L. McMahon
Abstract: The rapid growth in the size of state-of-the-art machine-learning (ML) models has led to increasing compute demands, spurring the development of energy-efficient hardware accelerators. Physical systems trained to perform ML inference, known as physical neural networks, have been proposed as accelerators in part because of their potential to be highly energy efficient. However, the ML inference accuracies reported in most investigations of physical neural networks have been substantially below the state of the art for conventional digital neural networks. In this work, we show in simulation studies that by applying modern ML training practices, including model compression, various candidate physical neural networks based on nonlinear dynamical systems can achieve accuracies as high as 99.2% on MNIST handwritten-digit recognition. We also show for one dynamical system that using it as the core building block of a parameter-efficient ML architecture inspired by MLP-Mixers enables accuracies of up to 86.4% on CIFAR-10 image classification (92.2% with pretraining and knowledge distillation), compared with 64% for the same system without the mixer architecture. The mixer approach achieved its accuracy while using $>5\times$ fewer trainable parameters than the baseline. Finally, we present back-of-the-envelope estimates for inference energy efficiency and speed when using an example physical dynamical system, suggesting that orders of magnitude improvements may be possible over digital-electronic neural networks.

### Paper [227]
Title: Single-Temperature Ranked Greedy Cluster Exchange for Max-Cut on Two-Dimensional Tori
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40515
Authors: Hao Geng, Nan Su, Gabriele Cavallaro
Abstract: Ground-state search in two-dimensional Ising spin glasses on large toroidal lattices remains challenging for local-update heuristics because rugged energy barriers impede relaxation across metastable states. Equivalent to zero-field toroidal Max-Cut problems, these spin-glass instances also serve as standard benchmarks for optimization heuristics. Many Monte Carlo approaches combine single-spin flips or cluster moves with temperature ladders or annealing schedules, yet even with tuned parameters, rarely achieve rapid, reliable ground-state convergence at scale. Here, we address the need for faster ground-state search through cooperation between replicas. We introduce ranked greedy cluster exchange (RGCX) and combine it with fixed-temperature Metropolis sweeps to form single-temperature RGCX. Replicas are ranked by cut to determine the pair-processing order. Each pair deterministically exchanges every disagreement cluster that raises the better replica's cut, retaining the complement and conserving the pair's summed cut. On the 20,000-spin Gset instance G81, the estimated runtime to reach the certified optimum with 99% success probability (TTS99) was 12.1 s on an 8-core desktop computer. With the same population size, exchange interval and temperature, the method reached the best known cuts on all 17 toroidal Gset graphs, including 9 certified optima. These findings advance ground-state search on large two-dimensional tori by establishing single-temperature RGCX as an efficient population-based method that combines ranked greedy cluster exchange with fixed-temperature Metropolis dynamics.

### Paper [228]
Title: Algebraic strong-to-weak symmetry breaking and partial decodability in the surface code
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40567
Authors: Stephen W. Yan, Yimu Bao, Sagar Vijay
Abstract: Coherent noise can give rise to unconventional regimes of quantum information recovery and mixed-state order. We study these phenomena in two settings related by Wegner duality: the triangular-lattice surface code subject to single-qubit Pauli-$Z$ rotations followed by syndrome measurements, and a $\mathbb{Z}_2$ paramagnet subject to strongly symmetric decoherence. Increasing the rotation angle drives a transition from a decodable phase to a \emph{partially decodable} critical phase in the code, and from a strongly symmetric phase to a phase with \emph{algebraic} $\mathbb{Z}_2$ strong-to-weak spontaneous symmetry breaking (SWSSB) in the dual paramagnet. We develop a common description of these phenomena in terms of a weakly coupled $\mathrm{SO}(n)$ nonlinear sigma model in the replica limit $n\to1$. Taking this replica limit analytically, we derive universal signatures of the critical phase: an optimal Pauli decoding fidelity that approaches a universal function of the system's aspect ratio in the thermodynamic limit, and a dual fidelity correlator that decays as a universal power law. These observables relate to correlations of twist operators in the sigma model description. Large-scale numerical simulations confirm these analytical predictions. We also argue that the algebraic SWSSB phase cannot be realized by any Pauli dephasing channel acting on the $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ paramagnet. We probe this "non-classical" character through the output state's sensitivity to well-separated $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ fluxes inserted in the initial state, which reveals persistent coherence between distinct $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ charge trajectories at long distances.

### Paper [229]
Title: Toric codes all the way down: decoupling translation-invariant topological CSS codes in higher dimensions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40592
Authors: Mingxin He, Yifei Wang, Hao Song, Yingfei Gu
Abstract: Topological Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) codes in more than two spatial dimensions offer features useful for fault-tolerant quantum computation, such as transversal non-Clifford gates and single-shot decoding, but a systematic understanding of them is still lacking. One approach is to decouple a code: map it to a stack of higher-form toric codes by a finite-depth local CSS-preserving Clifford circuit, possibly after adding ancillas. For translation-invariant $p$-ary CSS codes, where $p$ is prime ($p=2$ for qubits), we require the circuit to commute with the translations of a superlattice. In two dimensions, such a decoupling exists once these translations act trivially on every anyon type. In higher dimensions, excitations are sorted by their dimension into superselection layers, which can remain glued together even when translations act trivially on each layer. We show that this gluing is the only remaining obstruction: a code can be decoupled on a superlattice exactly when, after coarse-graining to it, translations act trivially on every layer and the gluing vanishes. Moreover, for a code with finitely many excitation types in each layer and $m$ nonzero layers, once translations act trivially on every layer, further coarse-graining by a factor of $p^{m-1}$ in every direction removes the aforementioned gluing. Physically, the same criterion determines when the code encodes the maximal number of logical qudits on a finite torus. Finally, we give an explicit algorithm that constructs the decoupling as a circuit of SUM (qudit CNOT) and SWAP gates.

### Paper [230]
Title: Probing fluctuations in photon emission statistics of spin injected quantum dots with time dependent electronic excitation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40593
Authors: Chiran Wijesundara, Vincent Iglesias-Cardinale, Yuan Lu, Herbert Fotso, Bo Xu, Tim Thomay
Abstract: Electrically spin-injected ensembles of InGaAs/GaAs quantum dots emit circularly polarized single photons, with the emission helicity determined by the spin polarization of the injected electrons. A ferromagnetic CoFeB spin-injection layer with perpendicular magnetic anisotropy, combined with an MgO tunnel barrier, enables spin-polarized electron injection into the quantum-dot active region, resulting in circularly polarized photon emission at zero applied magnetic field. These devices realize direct spin-charge-photon conversion in an electrically driven, microelectronics-compatible quantum light source that operates without an external pump laser. Using an extended Hanbury Brown-Twiss interferometer with four detectors and time-tagged readout, we measure time-resolved photon-number statistics that resolve up to $|2\rangle$ Fock states within a single excitation cycle. While single-photon ($n=|1\rangle$) emission tracks the electrical excitation pulse as expected, the two-photon ($n=|2\rangle$) statistics exhibit pronounced fluctuations at multiple temporal positions within the pulse. These fluctuations vanish upon demagnetization of the spin-injection layer, establishing a direct link between the multi-photon emission dynamics and the spin polarization of the injected carriers. The observations indicate that spin injection controls not only the helicity of the emitted photons but also higher-order temporal correlations of the emission. The behavior is phenomenologically captured by inserting both a variable excitation mechanism and a set of multi-emitter collapse operators within a stochastic wave function evolution. The platform thus opens a route to electrically driven, spin-controlled single and multi-photon Fock-state sources and provides a testbed for spin-conditioned multi-emitter dynamics in solid-state quantum light sources.

### Paper [231]
Title: On Zeros and Algorithms for Disordered Systems II: The Antiferromagnetic Ising Model up to Kesten-Stigum
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40598
Authors: Ferenc Bencs, Brice Huang, Daniel Z. Lee, Kuikui Liu, Guus Regts
Abstract: We give the first polynomial-time algorithm to estimate the partition function of the antiferromagnetic Ising model on the uniformly random $d$-regular graph to arbitrarily high accuracy for all inverse temperatures up to the Kesten-Stigum threshold. This is the entire regime in which the model is "replica symmetric," and is conjectured to be optimal for polynomial-time counting and sampling. To achieve our algorithmic result, we establish the absence of Fisher zeros for the partition function in a constant-width strip around the interval from $0$ up to the Kesten-Stigum threshold, with high probability over the randomness of the input graph.
  AI Disclosure: The overarching vision and proof architecture were developed solely by the authors without AI input. GPT 5.5 Pro was used, with extensive guidance from the authors, for only a few minor technical aspects of the proof. The authors independently verified correctness and wrote everything by hand. We take full responsibility for its contents. A more detailed statement can be found in Section 1.2.

### Paper [232]
Title: Exceptional Points as Witnesses of Non-Gaussian Noise
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40612
Authors: Aritra Ghosh, Subhashish Banerjee, M. Bhattacharya
Abstract: Efficient control of quantum devices requires the knowledge of their environmental noise, yet the commonly measured power spectrum does not fully characterize non-Gaussian fluctuations of realistic environments. Here we show that exceptional points (EPs) in the noise-averaged dephasing response can expose observable signatures of non-Gaussian statistics, distinguishing environments with identical lower-order statistics. We also introduce a theoretical model that describes dephasing environments in terms of identical independent noise sources while preserving the power spectrum. Given a calibrated single-source model, the largest EP order extracted from the measured dephasing response determines the number of sources and predicts the higher-order statistics of the combined noise. We illustrate an atomtronic readout that maps distinct EP signatures onto an evolving ensemble-averaged atomic interference pattern. Our results connect non-Hermitian dynamics to environmental noise statistics, providing a route to characterize noise in quantum devices used in computing, metrology, and precision measurement.

### Paper [233]
Title: Locality in Dissipative Ground-State Preparation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40628
Authors: Qicheng Tang, Ruhanshi Barad, Xueda Wen
Abstract: Can the ground state of a local Hamiltonian be prepared by local dissipation? We address this question for free-fermion Hamiltonians, using a quadratic Lindbladian with linear jump operators for the dissipative ground-state preparation. For translation-invariant targets, Fourier correspondence relates spatial locality to momentum-space analyticity, allowing us to derive a complete spectral criterion for the existence of exponentially local linear jump operators that prepare the ground state. This criterion leads to a no-go theorem that, sign-changing Fermi-level crossings exclude exponentially local linear jump operators for preparing the ground state. While these crossings are the complete obstruction in one dimension, additional obstructions generally arise in higher dimensions. We illustrate these additional obstructions using two quadratic-band-touching models with algebraically decaying ground-state correlations. We further show that short-range ground-state correlations, although not necessary, are sufficient for local dissipative preparation even without translation invariance. Our results establish how the spectral structure of the target Hamiltonian governs the locality of dissipative ground-state preparation.

### Paper [234]
Title: Entanglement diagnostics of stochastic-resetting steady states: theory and measurement on superconducting qubits
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40638
Authors: Jakob Murauer, Sabine Tornow, Manas Kulkarni, Eli Barkai, Satya N. Majumdar, Monalisa Singh Roy
Abstract: Stochastic resetting drives quantum systems into nonequilibrium stationary states unreachable under uninterrupted unitary evolution, and their entanglement depends on whether the reset history is resolved or averaged over. We study this interplay in the transverse-field Ising chain, deriving closed-form results for two qubits and evaluating chains of up to 12 qubits exactly. In the two-qubit system, these closed-form results show that phase matching between energy eigenstates produces resonances, and their visibility in the stationary state is determined by the symmetry of the reset state. Measurements on an IBM superconducting processor show the entropies evaluated before and after the reset average closely following noiseless predictions with no fitted parameter, while concurrence lies below prediction across most of the hardware measurements. The two diagnostics respond differently because they read different components of the same reconstructed state, and a depolarizing model captures the concurrence suppression with one fitted parameter. The difference between the two entropies, i. e., the Jensen gap of entanglement entropy, equals the Holevo information that a subsystem retains about the time since the last reset. With a Bell-state reset, this information grows with chain length across a half-chain cut over the sizes studied, without visible saturation at the smaller reset probabilities. Since reset times are sampled and recorded classically, the trajectory-averaged entropy, and with it the Jensen gap, is obtained without postselection, in contrast to monitored circuits, where trajectory-resolved entanglement requires sampling exponentially rare measurement records.

### Paper [235]
Title: Dynamic Peach-Koehler self-force, inertia, and radiation damping of a regularized dislocation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2005.12704
Authors: Yves-Patrick Pellegrini
Abstract: The elastodynamic Peach-Koehler force is computed for a fully-regularized straight dislocation with isotropic core in continuum isotropic elastic elasticity, in compact forms involving partial mass or impulsion functions relative to shear and compressional waves. The force accounts for both dynamic radiation damping and inertia. The expressions are valid indifferently for subsonic or supersonic velocities. Results are compared with the case of a flat-core dislocation of the Peierls-Eshelby type, for a motion of jump from rest to constant velocity. In the steady-state limit, the Lagrangian function relevant to expressing the force in the flat-core case must be replaced by a related but different function for the regularized dislocation. However, by suitably defining the regularizing dislocation width, the steady-state limits of the force for the fully-regularized and flat-core dislocations can be matched exactly.

### Paper [236]
Title: Disorder as a route to topological spin textures in amorphous magnets
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.07725
Authors: Temuujin Bayaraa, Subhashree Satapathy, Peter Fischer, Frances Hellman, Sin\'ead M. Griffin
Abstract: Topological phases in solids are typically associated with crystalline symmetry and periodic order. Recent work has shown, however, that reciprocal-space topology can persist even in amorphous materials. Whether real-space topology, encoded directly in magnetic textures, can emerge without crystalline symmetry remains unresolved. Here, we show that structural disorder itself can generate the interactions required to stabilize topological spin textures in amorphous FeGe. Using first-principles calculations to construct a statistical magnetic Hamiltonian, we find that local structural asymmetry and broad distributions of exchange couplings produce a frustrated magnetic network with net ferromagnetic character rather than spin-glass freezing, supporting vortex-, antivortex-, and skyrmion-like textures that self-organize into emergent spin superstructures. Monte Carlo simulations reproduce the experimental Curie temperature and show that these textures persist even when Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya and anisotropy terms are suppressed. In contrast, homogenizing the Hamiltonian strongly suppresses the topological response, identifying bond-to-bond heterogeneity as the essential ingredient. Our results show that real-space topology in a magnet need not be inherited from crystalline chirality, but can emerge from disorder itself.

### Paper [237]
Title: GAnnET: A principled approach to automatically annotating charge stability diagrams
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.11206
Authors: Brian Weber, Justyna P. Zwolak
Abstract: Gate-defined semiconductor quantum-dot (QD) arrays are a promising platform for quantum computing. However, their large configuration spaces and inherent measurement noise make device tuning time consuming and difficult to scale. Although machine-learning (ML) approaches for automated tuning have proliferated rapidly, a fair comparison of methods remains limited by inconsistent metrics and a lack of standardized labeled datasets. Here, we introduce Geometry-Informed ANNotator for Electrostatic Topologies (GAnnET), a physics- and geometry-informed algorithm that automatically generates probabilistic state labels for charge stability diagrams (CSDs) of double-QD systems. GAnnET constructs local geometric models of CSD structure and combines them into a global state map with calibrated confidence, eliminating the need for manual annotation. We validate GAnnET on simulated and experimental CSDs and demonstrate consistent labeling suitable for ML training and benchmarking. By enabling reproducible annotation of experimental data, GAnnET supports community benchmarking and scalable automated QD operation.

### Paper [238]
Title: Quantum Hall transformer in a quantum point contact over the full range of transmission
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.17778
Authors: Stuart Yi-Thomas, Jay D. Sau
Abstract: A recent experiment [Cohen et al., Science 382, 542 (2023)] observed a robustly quantized $e^2/2h$ conductance in a quantum point contact between fractional quantum Hall edges (a quantum Hall transformer), which then vanishes at low temperature. While this behavior can be described by a boundary sine gordon (BSG) model derived from electron tunneling, the microscopic motivation for such strong tunneling is unclear. We use an alternative model based on sliding charge density waves and a density inhomogeneity to clarify the BSG description and calculate the conductance over the full range of transmission. Using a perturbative method and a matrix product state calculation, we draw a quantitative connection between the BSG model and the physical quantum Hall system.

### Paper [239]
Title: Computational quantum transport: a scattering approach perspective
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.16257
Authors: Xavier Waintal, Michael Wimmer, Anton Akhmerov, Christoph Groth, Branislav K. Nikolic, Mathieu Istas, T\'omas \"Orn Rosdahl, Daniel Varjas
Abstract: This review is devoted to the different techniques that have been developed to compute the phase-coherent transport properties of quantum nanoelectronic systems connected to electrodes. Beside a review of the different algorithms proposed in the literature, we provide a comprehensive and pedagogical derivation of the two formalisms on which these techniques are based: the scattering approach and the (nonequilibrium) Green's function approach. We show that the scattering problem can be formulated as a system of linear equations and that different existing algorithms for solving this scattering problem amount to different sequences of Gaussian elimination. We explicitly prove the equivalence of the two formalisms. We discuss the stability and numerical complexity of the existing methods. The review ends with a selection of a few applications where numerical calculations were instrumental in shaping our understanding of the physics.

### Paper [240]
Title: Active morphodynamics of intracellular organelles in the trafficking pathway
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.19084
Authors: S. Alex Rautu, Richard G. Morris, Madan Rao
Abstract: From the Golgi apparatus to endosomes, organelles in the endomembrane system exhibit complex and varied morphologies that are often related to their function. These membrane-bound organelles operate far from equilibrium due to directed fluxes of trafficking vesicles, yet the physical principles governing the emergence and maintenance of these morphologies remain elusive. By coarse-graining the active mechanochemical cycles associated with individual fission and fusion events, we show how vesicular trafficking manifests at the hydrodynamic scale, generating not only fluxes of membrane area and encapsulated volume but also active stresses that mediate momentum transfer between an organelle and its cytosolic environment. The fluid and deformable nature of the bounding membrane gives rise to novel physics by coupling these nonequilibrium forces to organelle composition, morphology, and hydrodynamic flows. We demonstrate how both stable compartment drift and ramified sac-like morphologies, reminiscent of Golgi cisternal motion and shape, emerge from the same nonequilibrium dynamics of fission and fusion.

### Paper [241]
Title: Chiral Geometric Qubit in a Frustrated Molecular Spin Triangle
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.11964
Authors: Aman Ullah, Ziqi Hu, Juan Arag\'o, Sergey A. Varganov, Alejandro Gaita-Ari\~no
Abstract: Spin frustration in antiferromagnetically coupled triangles creates states distinguished by chirality, offering a molecular route to encoded quantum operations. Here, we show how scalar chirality and geometric phases arise in the ground doublet of a spin-$1/2$ triangle. A Dzyaloshinskii--Moriya (DM) interaction establishes the chirality of the states, while broken $C_3$ symmetry allows these states to acquire geometric phases. We show how to encode a qubit in states of opposite scalar chirality and opposite geometric phases and implement a geometric $Z$ gate that cancels the relative dynamical phase, making it insensitive to pulse-duration errors. As an example of such a chiral geometric qubit, we investigate a Cu$_3$-based paramagnetic molecular magnet, using broken-symmetry density functional theory and multireference spin--orbit calculations to estimate the strengths of the exchange and DM interactions. The results show that such paramagnetic molecules could serve as tunable platforms for implementing chiral geometric qubits.

### Paper [242]
Title: Quantum noise spectroscopy of superconducting dynamics in a cuprate thin film
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.04439
Authors: Zhongyuan Liu, Ruotian Gong, Jaewon Kim, Oriana K. Diessel, Jiajie Cheng, Qiaozhi Xu, Zackary Rehfuss, Xinyi Du, Guanghui He, Abhishek Singh, Yun Suk Eo, Erik A. Henriksen, G. D. Gu, Norman Y. Yao, Francisco Machado, Sheng Ran, Shubhayu Chatterjee, Chong Zu
Abstract: Characterizing the low-energy dynamics of quantum materials is crucial for understanding strongly correlated electronic states, yet remains experimentally challenging, particularly in two-dimensional systems. Here, we leverage nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in diamond as a non-invasive quantum sensor to study thin-film Bi$_2$Sr$_2$CaCu$_2$O$_{8+\delta}$ (BSCCO), revealing several distinct dynamical phenomena across the superconducting phase diagram. At zero magnetic field and low temperatures, NV depolarization ($T_1$) spectroscopy captures GHz-scale noise from nodal quasiparticle excitations, consistent with Bardeen--Cooper--Schrieffer (BCS) mean-field theory. Near the critical temperature $T_c \approx 90$ K, supercurrent-fluctuation-induced noise sharply reduces $T_1$. By analyzing the temperature scaling of $T_1$, we observe clear deviations from mean-field prediction, reflecting the importance of order parameter fluctuations and enabling the determination of critical exponents. Under a small magnetic field, a broad and asymmetric reduction of $T_1$ unveils a liquid-like phase of mobile vortices. NV decoherence ($T_2$) spectroscopy further probes MHz-scale noise and reveals complex vortex-solid fluctuations well below $T_c$. Our results establish quantum noise spectroscopy as a non-invasive probe of superconducting dynamics over frequency and length scales complementary to existing techniques.

### Paper [243]
Title: Long-range correlations in a locally constrained exclusion process
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.12632
Authors: Stefan Gro{\ss}kinsky, Gunter Sch\"utz, Ali Zahra
Abstract: We introduce a novel exclusion process with a simple local kinetic constraint that leads to a remarkable transition between a homogeneous phase with short-range correlations and a clustered phase with long-range correlations and spontaneous breaking of translation invariance. The metastable dynamics of particle clusters lead to a coarsening cascade and glassy dynamics, as well as an intriguing faster-is-slower effect where an increasing asymmetry in the flow direction leads to a decrease of the stationary current.

### Paper [244]
Title: Raman Circular Dichroism and spinon Quantum Geometry of Chiral Quantum Spin Liquids
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.14091
Authors: Eduard Koller, Valentin Leeb, Natalia B. Perkins, Johannes Knolle
Abstract: We show that the quantum geometry of fractionalized spin excitations in Mott-insulating quantum spin liquids (QSL) gives rise to a finite Raman circular dichroism (RCD) signal.
  We identify conditions under which the Loudon--Fleury framework, where light modulates the underlying exchange interactions, and the light-matter-coupling approach, where the effective vector potential couples directly to the spinon bands, yield equivalent Raman vertices for the fractionalized quasiparticles. Using the latter, we derive an analytical decomposition of the RCD into different quantum geometric contributions. This reveals the sensitivity of the RCD to the underlying structure of the wave functions and the handedness of the excitations, rather than a nonzero Chern number of spinon excitations. To illustrate this, we apply our approach to two examples, the Kitaev honeycomb model in a magnetic field and a parton mean-field state of the chiral QSL on the triangular lattice.

### Paper [245]
Title: LightPFP: A Lightweight Route to Ab Initio Accuracy at Scale
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.23064
Authors: Wenwen Li, Nontawat Charoenphakdee, Yong-Bin Zhuang, Ryuhei Okuno, Yuta Tsuboi, So Takamoto, Junichi Ishida, Iori Kurata, Ju Li
Abstract: Machine learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) bridge the gap between quantum-mechanical accuracy and scalable molecular simulations. However, a fundamental trade-off persists: universal MLIPs (u-MLIPs) offer broad transferability but are computationally expensive, whereas task-specific MLIPs (ts-MLIPs) achieve high efficiency at the expense of costly DFT training data. Here we introduce LightPFP, a data-efficient knowledge distillation framework. Instead of relying on expensive DFT calculations, LightPFP constructs a distilled ts-MLIP by using a u-MLIP to generate high-quality, material-specific training data, while employing a pre-trained lightweight model to further improve data efficiency. Across diverse material systems, LightPFP reduces model development time by approximately three orders of magnitude compared with DFT-based workflows, while maintaining DFT-level accuracy. Combined with a LAMMPS/Kokkos inference backend, LightPFP achieves more than three orders of magnitude higher inference efficiency than u-MLIP baselines while enabling simulations of systems thousands of times larger. When the teacher exhibits systematic bias, LightPFP supports few-shot precision correction using as few as 10 high-accuracy DFT configurations, as demonstrated by reproducing MgO's experimental melting point. This u-MLIP-driven distillation establishes a scalable route to high-fidelity, data-efficient MLIPs, accelerating the pace of materials discovery and design.

### Paper [246]
Title: Shape dependence of Edelstein and magnetoelectric effects in the V-shaped model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.14404
Authors: Shuhei Kanda, Satoru Hayami
Abstract: We theoretically investigate the shape dependence and microscopic mechanism of the magnetoelectric (ME) effect, including both nonmagnetic (Edelstein-type) and magnetic origins, in a V-shaped one-dimensional chain model. Numerical calculations based on the Kubo formula reveal that the nonmagnetic-driven ME response is maximized at an apex angle of $\theta \approx 0.6\pi$. To clarify its origin, we derive a low-energy effective Hamiltonian in the $s$-orbital subspace and demonstrate that the polarity induced by the V-shaped geometry manifests as an effective spin--orbit interaction. An analytical derivation of the Green's function shows that the geometric effect can be described as a $T$-matrix contribution associated with local symmetry breaking. This scattering-theoretical representation enables us to identify the contributions of local geometric symmetry breaking to the ME response. Using a multipole-basis representation, we identify symmetry-based selection rules for the ME tensor and show that the coupling between the effective spin--orbit interaction and the orbital angular momentum generated across the apex plays an essential role. The resulting angular dependence, $\sin{\theta}\sin{\theta/2}$, peaks at $\theta = 2\tan^{-1}\sqrt{2} \approx 0.608\pi$, in good agreement with the numerical results. We also analyze a ferromagnetic V-shaped model including the Zeeman interaction and show that the magnetic-driven ME response originates from the spin magnetization induced by the coupling between the electric-field--driven charge-potential gradient and the Zeeman term. These results reveal distinct ME mechanisms depending on the presence or absence of time-reversal symmetry and clarify the microscopic origin of shape-dependent ME responses in V-shaped chains.

### Paper [247]
Title: Crystal Orbital Guided Iteration to Atomic Orbitals: A Pathway to Chemically Adaptive Atomic Orbitals from DFT
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.19725
Authors: Emily Oliphant, Emmanouil Kioupakis, Wenhao Sun
Abstract: Atomic orbitals underpin our understanding of electronic structure, providing intuitive descriptions of bonding, charge transfer, magnetism, and correlation effects. Despite their utility, an atomic basis that is adaptable, strictly localized on atomic centers, and enables accurate tight-binding interpolation has remained elusive. Here, we introduce Crystal Orbital Guided Iteration To atomic-Orbitals (COGITO), a framework that constructs an optimal atomic orbital basis by identifying and resolving key mathematical obstacles inherent to nonorthogonal bases -- particularly uncontrolled orbital mixing, and the fixed-overlap constraint between orbitals. We demonstrate that COGITO enables tight-binding models as accurate as MLWF-based approaches, while preserving the ability of tight-binding parameters to represent the projected atomic basis -- an essential feature lost in schemes that enforce orbital orthogonality or maximal localization. By creating accurate and chemically interpretable models of electronic structure, COGITO reveals the orbital-resolved covalent bonds and charge transfer that is encoded in the Kohn-Sham wavefunctions of DFT. Our method thus offers a powerful tool for any physics- or chemistry-based application that relies on a faithful description of local electronic structure.

### Paper [248]
Title: Curvature-stabilized three-dimensional spiral states and local-field nucleation of skyrmions on spherical caps
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.22557
Authors: Sabri Koraltan, Joe Sunny, Emily Darwin, Daniel Rothhardt, Reshma Peremadathil-Pradeep, Micha{\l} Krupi\'nski, Takeaki Gokita, Robert Kraft, Claas Abert, Jakub Jurczyk, Amalio Fern\'andez-Pacheco, Markus Weigand, Sebastian Wintz, Dieter Suess, Hans Josef Hug, Manfred Albrecht
Abstract: The combination of polystyrene particles and multilayers with chiral interfacial interactions offers an interesting platform to study the interplay between topology, chirality, and curvature. Here, we investigate Pt/Co/Ta multilayers sputtered onto self-assembled polystyrene particles with a diameter of 919 nm, which show a magnetic reversal distinctly different from that of planar films. Using non-invasive scanning transmission X-ray microscopy and in-vacuum magnetic force microscopy (MFM) with low-moment tips, we observe maze domains in the planar films, whereas the curvilinear films host three-dimensional spiraling stripe states. Repeated scanning with a conventional higher-moment MFM tip ruptures these spirals into metastable skyrmions at the top of the particles. Micromagnetic simulations of spherical caps reveal that increasing curvature renders a single spiral stripe energetically favorable over an isolated skyrmion, and that this stabilization is predominantly of magnetostatic origin. Our results identify the spiral states as intrinsic to the curvilinear geometry and the skyrmions as metastable states nucleated by local magnetic fields, underlining the value of non-invasive imaging to distinguish intrinsic curvature effects from probe-induced magnetic textures.

### Paper [249]
Title: A First Look at Hydrogen Generation in an Ultramafic Rock with Micro-CT and SEM-BEX
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.09466
Authors: Hannah P. Menke, Zaid Z. Jangda, CJ Jones, Max Webb, Jim Buckman, Amy Gough
Abstract: We present a 4D (time-lapse) micro-CT experiment that visualizes gas emergence and growth during water-rock reaction in an ultramafic granular pack at 100$^\circ$C. Repeated scans resolve the onset of gas exsolution after an initial delay, followed by rapid bubble growth, coalescence, and evolving gas connectivity. Image-derived metrics quantify changes in bulk gas saturation and gas clustering through time. Hydrogen was detected independently in the pump effluent and by an indicator strip in the confining sleeve. A companion reactor experiment using the same parent rock at 100$^\circ$C yielded an estimated net production of 0.65 mmol H$_2$ over approximately three weeks, accounting for dissolved hydrogen and gas removed during sampling. Complementary SEM and multi-element backscattered electron and X-ray (BEX) imaging of pre- and post-reaction grains indicates localized alteration textures consistent with reaction-driven H$_2$ production. These observations constrain pore-scale gas accumulation and multiphase transport relevant to natural hydrogen accumulations.

### Paper [250]
Title: Point-Group Equivariant Neural Networks for Local Dynamics in Lattice Systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.04104
Authors: Yunhao Fan, Gia-Wei Chern
Abstract: We develop point-group equivariant neural networks for learning local mappings used in dynamical simulations of lattice systems. The same network acts on a finite neighborhood around each site, respecting lattice translations through shared parameters. The local configuration is decomposed into features that transform according to irreducible representations of the point group, and hidden features retain this organization throughout the network. Equivariant linear maps and symmetry-allowed tensor products then learn nonlinear spatial correlations while preserving the required transformation laws. This approach avoids constructing a fixed set of hand-crafted invariant descriptors, whose selection requires choices about which local correlations to retain. It also allows the complexity of the learned mapping to grow without enlarging its spatial cutoff. We demonstrate the construction by learning the forces governing adiabatic dynamics in the square-lattice Holstein model. Comparisons with exact diagonalization test the accuracy of the predicted forces and the evolution of spatial correlations. Force evaluation scales linearly with system size, allowing the trained model to simulate charge-density-wave ordering on large lattices. These simulations reproduce anomalously slow coarsening of the charge-density-wave domains, illustrating the use of the learned local mapping for collective dynamics.

### Paper [251]
Title: Kineo-Elasticity and Nonreciprocal Phonons by Rashba-induced Interfacial Spin-Lattice Coupling
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12656
Authors: Gyungchoon Go, Se Kwon Kim
Abstract: We identify a previously unrecognized spin-lattice coupling allowed by broken inversion symmetry that can be viewed as a lattice analogue of electronic Rashba spin-orbit coupling. In the low-frequency regime, where magnons are integrated out, its interplay with the conventional magnetoelastic coupling generates a kineo-elastic term that couples strain and lattice velocity, giving rise to nonreciprocal phonon velocity. We further analyze the full magnon-phonon spectrum and find that the competition between interfacial spin-lattice and magnetoelastic couplings leads to directional hybridization and absorption, yielding asymmetric phonon propagation lengths for opposite directions. Our results indicate that such interfacial spin-lattice coupling can provide an efficient route to nonreciprocal phonon propagation in magnetic heterostructures with strong Rashba spin-orbit coupling.

### Paper [252]
Title: Rheologically tuned diffusion modulates quorum sensing in Vibrio fischeri
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01786
Authors: Chunhe Li, Zixiang Lin, Hongyi Bian, Anqi Li, Honyi Xin, Zijie Qu
Abstract: Understanding how the physical properties of a fluid influence bacterial behavior is essential for explaining how microorganisms interact with their environment and with animal hosts. Here, we examine how changes in fluid viscosity and rheological properties affect the locomotion of the marine bacterium Vibrio fischeri and its ability to produce luminescence through cell--cell communication. We track the three-dimensional motion of single cells in well-defined fluids with different physical properties and measure the luminescence emitted by cell populations. We find that fluids with higher viscosity cause V. fischeri to spend more time in a slower, turning-focused swimming mode, which reduces how effectively cells spread out and encounter the chemical signals required to activate luminescence. As a result, luminescence first increases and then decreases in Newtonian fluids, but decreases monotonically in fluids that exhibit non-Newtonian rheological behavior. Computer simulations based on our measurements confirm that the ability of cells to explore their surroundings plays a central role in determining when and how strongly they communicate. These findings reveal a direct link between the physical environment, bacterial movement, and collective behavior, and offer insight into how microorganisms adapt to complex fluid habitats, including those found inside animal hosts.

### Paper [253]
Title: Giant bubbles of Fisher zeros in the quantum XY chain
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05899
Authors: Songtai Lv, Yang Liu, Erhai Zhao, Haiyuan Zou, Tao Xiang
Abstract: We demonstrate an alternative approach based on a complex-valued inverse temperature and partition function to probe quantum phases of matter with nontrivial spectra and dynamics. It leverages thermofield dynamics (TFD) to quantitatively characterize quantum and thermal fluctuations, and exploit the correspondence between low-energy excitations and Fisher zeros. Using the quantum XY chain in an external field as a testbed, we show that the oscillatory gap behavior manifests as oscillations in the long-time dynamics of the TFD spectral form factor. We also identify giant bubbles, i.e. large-scale closed lines, of Fisher zeros near the gapless XX limit. They provide a characteristic energy scale that seems to contradict the predictions of the low energy theory of a featureless Luttinger liquid. We identify this energy scale and relate the motion of these giant bubbles with varying external field to the transfer of spectral weight from high to low energies. The deep connection between Fisher zeros, dynamics, and excitations opens up promising avenues for understanding the unconventional gap behaviors in strongly correlated many-body systems.

### Paper [254]
Title: Reducing the Computational Cost Scaling of Tensor Network Algorithms via Field-Programmable Gate Array Parallelism
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05900
Authors: Songtai Lv, Yang Liang, Rui Zhu, Qibin Zheng, Haiyuan Zou
Abstract: Improving the computational efficiency of quantum many-body calculations from a hardware perspective remains a critical challenge. Although field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs) have recently been exploited to improve the computational scaling of algorithms such as Monte Carlo methods, their application to tensor network algorithms is still at an early stage. In this work, we propose a fine-grained parallel tensor network design based on FPGAs to substantially enhance the computational efficiency of two representative tensor network algorithms: the infinite time-evolving block decimation (iTEBD) and the higher-order tensor renormalization group (HOTRG). By employing a quad-tile partitioning strategy to decompose tensor elements and map them onto hardware circuits, our approach effectively translates algorithmic computational complexity into scalable hardware resource utilization, enabling an extremely high degree of parallelism on FPGAs. Compared with conventional CPU-based implementations, our scheme exhibits superior scalability in computation time, reducing the bond-dimension scaling of the computational cost from $O(D_b^3)$ to $O(D_b)$ for iTEBD and from $O(D_b^6)$ to $O(D_b^2)$ for HOTRG. This work provides a theoretical foundation for future hardware implementations of large-scale tensor network computations.

### Paper [255]
Title: Spontaneous Parity Breaking in Quantum Antiferromagnets on the Triangular Lattice
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05901
Authors: Songtai Lv, Yuchen Meng, Haiyuan Zou
Abstract: Frustration on the triangular lattice has long been a source of intriguing and often debated phases in many-body systems. Although symmetry analysis has been employed, the role of the seemingly trivial parity symmetry has received little attention. In the work described in this Letter, we show that phases induced by frustration are systematically shaped by an implicit rule of thumb associated with spontaneous parity breaking in weak longitudinal field. This principle enables us to anticipate and rationalize the regimes and conditions under which nontrivial phases emerge. For the spin-$S$ antiferromagnetic XXZ model, we demonstrate that a controversial parity-broken phase appears at intermediate values of $S$. In bilayer systems, enhanced frustration leads to additional phases, such as supersolids, whose properties can be classified by their characteristic parity features. Benefiting from our improved tensor network contraction techniques, we confirm these results through large-scale tensor-network calculations. This study offers an alternative viewpoint and a systematic approach for examining the interplay between spin, symmetry, and frustration in many-body systems.

### Paper [256]
Title: Thermal Strain-Driven Periodic Ripples in Ultrathin Sb2Te3/Graphene Heterostructures and their Implications for Strain Engineering
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11932
Authors: Francisco Munoz, Manuel Fuenzalida, Paula Mellado, Caleb Z. Zerger, Hari C. Manoharan, Valentina Gallardo, Carolina Parra
Abstract: We report the observation of a highly regular, periodic ripple superstructure with a characteristic wavelength of approximately 8.7 nm in a van der Waals heterostructure composed of ultrathin Sb2Te3 epitaxially grown on monolayer graphene. We attribute this structural modulation to an extrinsic, thermally driven mechanism: upon post-growth cooling, the opposite signs of the thermal expansion coefficients of the bulk SiO2 substrate and the graphene layer generate interfacial strain that drives the formation of ripples. In this ultrathin thickness regime, the Sb2Te3 film conforms to the buckled graphene morphology without delamination, because the interfacial van der Waals adhesion energy exceeds the elastic energy cost associated with bending. This substrate-induced templating mechanism is expected to be broadly applicable to other two-dimensional materials grown on graphene, provided that they are sufficiently thin to remain mechanically coupled below a critical bending thickness.
