The field-mixing parameter is $r=-1.585(19)$, while the coexistence-line slope gives $r=-1.587(5)$. Normalizing by the zero-temperature field $\Delta_0=zJ/2$, the results for honeycomb, square, and triangular lattices give $r/\Delta_0$ between $-1.057$ and $-1.067$, about 6% larger in magnitude than the mean-field value. Simple-cubic estimates are compatible with this range but do not resolve dimensionality dependence. Finite-size scaling yields $y_t=1.795(7)$, consistent with the tricritical Ising value $9/5$, while the fourth-order cumulant scaling agrees with $y_g=4/5$. Deep in the first-order regime, the cumulant approaches the two-phase limit $U_Q\to2/3$. Multicanonical simulations up to $L=64$ yield an interface tension exponent $\mu=1.265(26)$, consistent with the tricritical prediction $(d-1)/y_g=5/4$. We also map the continuous phase boundary, obtaining $T_c(\Delta=0)=1.157$, and identify crossover effects from Ising to tricritical scaling near the tricritical point.

### Paper [309]
Title: Benchmarking Universal Machine-Learning Interatomic Potentials for Temperature-Dependent Elasticity of Binary and High-Entropy Refractory Carbides
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11878
Authors: Miroslav Lebeda, Jan Drahokoupil, \v{S}imon Svoboda, Petr Vl\v{c}\'ak
Abstract: Accurate predictions of temperature-dependent stiffness are essential for assessing the high-temperature performance of refractory carbides, yet the cost of ab initio molecular dynamics (AIMD) limits systematic exploration across compositions and temperatures. Universal machine-learning interatomic potentials (uMLIPs) offer a computationally efficient alternative, but their reliability for finite-temperature elasticity remains insufficiently established. Here, we benchmark nine uMLIPs against consistent AIMD reference data for five binary and two high-entropy (HE) carbides between 300 and 1200 K. Elastic constants and the corresponding bulk, shear, and Young's moduli are obtained using stress-strain molecular dynamics, explicitly sampling thermal atomic motion and anharmonic effects beyond thermal expansion alone. We assess absolute elastic properties and normalized thermal softening separately. MACE-MH-1 achieves the lowest overall mean absolute percentage error (5.7%). MACE-MH-1 and DPA4-Mini reproduce thermal softening most accurately, with mean deviations of 2.5 and 2.3 percentage points, respectively. All models generally underestimate stiffness, with larger equilibrium volumes relative to the AIMD reference likely contributing to this trend for most models. C12 exhibits the largest model-dependent errors. The HE carbides are described with accuracy comparable to that of the binary carbides, indicating no apparent accuracy penalty from chemical complexity. Accuracy instead varies with transition-metal composition, with group-V carbides, particularly TaC, presenting the greatest challenge. These results identify promising pretrained models for finite-temperature elasticity in carbides and show why accurate absolute stiffness and thermal softening must be assessed independently.

### Paper [310]
Title: Competitive scaling of small-subsystem-optimization heuristics for LABS
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14361
Authors: Ginger E. Lau, Adil A. Gangat
Abstract: The low autocorrelation binary sequences (LABS) problem, often formulated as a spin-lattice energy-minimization problem, is a combinatorial optimization problem with a very rugged energy landscape. We demonstrate that iterative optimization of very small subsystems of n spins that are decoupled from the rest of the system achieves global optimization of LABS with a time-to-solution scaling that is competitive with heuristics that optimize the global system of N spins directly. Our isolated-subsystem heuristic with n = 4 achieves a time scaling of O(1.33^N ) on the interval N = [27, 60], while the current state-of-the-art classical and quantum scalings are respectively O(1.34^N ) and O(1.21^N ). Our results suggest that specialized optimization hardware with only small numbers of logical spins may be of practical relevance, and that quantum speedups demonstrated in the absence of decomposition may not provide a practical quantum advantage for large-scale problems that require decomposition.

### Paper [311]
Title: Topological Chiral Superconductivity from Doping Correlated Charge Transfer Insulator with Orbital Magnetism
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16674
Authors: Wen Sun, Yuan Yang, Shou-Shu Gong, Wen Huang
Abstract: Topological chiral superconductivity has been extensively studied from the perspective of electronic structures in momentum space, while its emergence from real-space processes in strongly interacting systems remains largely unexplored. Here, we study a doped charge-transfer insulator in a strongly repulsive spinless-fermion model with three sublattices and $2\pi$ flux per unit cell. Combining perturbative analysis, exact diagonalization, and density matrix renormalization group simulations, we establish a topological superconducting ground state with $p+ip$ symmetry. In the large charge-transfer gap limit, chiral charge-transfer excitations generate a correlated-hopping process that provides a microscopic mechanism for electron pairing and its chiral structure. The chiral superconductivity persists into the smaller-gap regime, where chiral excitonic two-particle bound state can emerge. Our results establish a concrete real-space route to topological chiral superconductivity upon doping strongly interacting charge-transfer insulators with orbital magnetism.

### Paper [312]
Title: Beyond the lemon: Spectra of random Lindblad operators with a Hamiltonian component
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19261
Authors: Mykhailo Hontarenko, Wojciech Tarnowski, Sergey Denisov, Karol \.Zyczkowski
Abstract: Generators of continuous-time quantum Markovian evolution, known as Lindblad operators, play a key role in the theory of open quantum systems. The question of how to define and characterize random Lindblad operators has attracted considerable attention in recent years. Purely dissipative random Lindbladians have been extensively studied using toolboxes of free probability and non-Hermitian random matrix theories, which helped to uncover a characteristic lemon-like spectral support. In this work, we focus on random Lindbladians with a Hamiltonian component. We analyze three ensembles of increasing complexity, designed to illustrate the joint effects of dissipative and Hamiltonian dynamics. While the first two allow for an exact analytic treatment with standard probabilistic and scalar-valued free-probability methods, the most general case requires the more general framework of operator-valued free probability. We explain how the spectral support changes with the relative Hamiltonian strength and the rank of the dissipative component and characterize the effect of Hamiltonian-induced spectral squeezing.

### Paper [313]
Title: Spectral signatures of spin textures and the emergence of a pseudogap
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19269
Authors: Matteo Quinzi, Andrea Pintus, Tommaso Chiarotti, Massimo Capone, Nicola Marzari
Abstract: Strongly correlated solids exhibit phenomena -- from Mott localization to high-temperature superconductivity -- that escape conventional band theory. Efforts to address this unconventional physics have been typically cast in the language of Green's function theories, with the associated self-energies capturing strong correlations beyond the reach of mean-field theories. Here, we underscore that frequency- and momentum-dependent self-energies emerge also from mean-field solutions that break translational symmetry, and that these self-energies can display the characteristic fingerprints of strong correlations for certain, physically meaningful, spatial patterns. In the paradigmatic case of the 2D Hubbard model, we find that already at the mean-field level the doped antiferromagnet develops, in addition to long-range ordered stripes, many quasi-degenerate self-consistent solutions with antiferromagnetic textures organized into antiphase domains separated by hole-rich interfaces of convoluted shape, reminiscent of the phase-separation scenario. Remarkably, these solutions map into spectral functions that display very distinctively the phenomenology of the pseudogap, including the nodal-antinodal dichotomy, Fermi arcs, and a complementary Luttinger surface. The result is a conceptually simple description of such a complex phenomenon, where a pseudogap emerges as the momentum-space spectral portrait of the real-space interplay between antiferromagnetic domains and their metallic interfaces.

### Paper [314]
Title: Solving fractional quantum Hall problems with thermodynamic-limit bootstrap
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21317
Authors: Qiang Gao, Taige Wang
Abstract: Determining correlated ground states directly in the thermodynamic limit remains a central challenge in quantum many-body physics. Here we develop a quantum many-body bootstrap that yields a rigorous and tight lower bound on the thermodynamic ground-state energy of a fractional quantum Hall system together with an optimized reduced density matrix $\rho_W^\star$ on a spatial window. For sufficiently tight bounds, we find that $\rho_W^\star$ accurately reproduces the ground-state reduced density matrix, as directly tested by its fidelity with known model states. For exactly solvable Laughlin and Moore--Read parent Hamiltonians, the bootstrap also reproduces pair correlations and orbital entanglement spectra (OES). For Landau-level-projected Coulomb interactions across multiple fillings, the energy bounds closely track established thermodynamic estimates, while the window density matrix recovers the familiar $\nu=1/3$ Laughlin and $\nu=5/2$ Moore--Read states and the composite Fermi liquid at $\nu=1/2$. More importantly, the same framework also resolves unsettled regimes. At $\nu=1/4$, the OES and real-space correlations identify a composite Fermi liquid, while at $\nu=12/5$ the combined diagnostics suggest $\overline{\mathrm{RR}}_3$ as the ground-state order. Our bootstrap therefore provides direct access to both ground-state energies and many-body structure in the thermodynamic limit.

### Paper [315]
Title: From Action Principle to Stochastic Thermodynamics of Elastic and Viscous Active Matter
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21835
Authors: Valentina Barletta, Michele Lupini, Ian D. Estabrook, Laeschkir W\"urthner, Isabella R. Graf, Caren Norden, Lamberto Rondoni, Anna Erzberger, Roman Belousov
Abstract: Motivated by the complex material properties of living matter, we derive a framework of stochastic thermo- and elastodynamics for active materials from the classical principle of least action and the Rayleighian formalism for external forces. Our approach couples the local barycentric collective coordinate and the streaming velocity as the canonical conjugate pair, which unifies various descriptions of elastic and viscous soft matter, including Helfrich theory for lipid membranes, linear and nonlinear elasticity of thin shells and deformable droplets, as well as hydrodynamics of active and passive fluids. The Lagrangian mechanics of collective field variables permits direct applications of the results and theorems from statistical mechanics to existing models of complex soft and living matter. To illustrate the potential applications of stochastic elastodynamics in biology, we derive and simulate a boundary-driven deformation model of an elastic cell nucleus in the presence of fluctuations.

### Paper [316]
Title: Maximum-Temperature Theorem for a Nanothermometer
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24340
Authors: Taras Plakhotnik
Abstract: We derive a sharp upper bound on the quasi-steady temperature rise of a sensor of arbitrary shape and known thermometric volume $V_\mathrm{s}$, placed anywhere inside a region enclosed by an isothermal reference surface. The bound also requires the total heat power generated in the region $\dot{Q}_\mathrm{r}$ and a lower bound on its thermal conductivity $\kappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal {V}_{\mathrm{r}}}}$. It is otherwise independent of the heat-source distribution, domain shape, and source--sensor distance. For $\dot Q_\mathrm{r}=50~\mathrm{pW}$, $\kappa_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm r}}} =0.1~\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}K^{-1}}$, and $V_{\mathrm s}$ equal to the volume of a $60~\mathrm{nm}$-diameter sphere, the theorem permits a maximum temperature rise of approximately $2~\mathrm{mK}$. Neither source localization nor uncertain source--sensor separation can therefore account for reported kelvin-scale intracellular readings under steady-state heat conduction. The result applies to sensors based on
  nanodiamonds, upconversion nanoparticles, fluorescent polymer beads, and core--shell quantum dots.

### Paper [317]
Title: Theory of High-Temperature Fractional Chern Phases in Monolayer van der Waals Kagome Materials A$_2$T$_3$X$_4$
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24633
Authors: Soomin Oh, Jiyong Han, Jinhyeok Choi, Donghae Seo, Mingu Kang, Gil Young Cho
Abstract: Fractional Chern insulators bring the fractional quantum Hall effect, with its anyonic excitations, to zero magnetic field. So far they have been realized in moir\'e materials, where the long superlattice period weakens electronic interactions and confines these phases to cryogenic temperatures. Early proposals instead envisioned topological flat bands on atomic-scale lattices, where fractionalization could persist to far higher temperatures, but a suitable material has been lacking. Here we theoretically identify monolayers of the van der Waals kagome family $\mathrm{A}_2\mathrm{T}_3\mathrm{X}_4$ as such a platform, combining intrinsic topological flat bands and strong Coulomb interactions with gate-tunable carrier density and layer assembly. Exact diagonalization of models derived from density functional theory reveals ferromagnetism over broad filling ranges, supporting diverse fractional Chern insulators, anomalous composite Fermi liquids, and charge-ordered phases. In particular, in $\mathrm{Cs}_2\mathrm{Pd}_3\mathrm{S}_4$, the fractional Chern insulator exhibits a many-body gap of approximately 26 meV in the largest system accessible with band mixing allowing at most one interband electron-hole excitation. Layer assembly further provides a natural route to fractional quantum spin Hall insulators: bilayers with antiparallel magnetizations support this phase at realistic separations. These results identify an experimentally accessible material family in which chemical composition, carrier density, and stacking offer complementary routes to fractionalization at enhanced energy scales and control over topological order.

### Paper [318]
Title: Scaling Dimensions and Two-Point Amplitudes from Operator Covariance Matrices
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24943
Authors: You-Chiuan Chen, Ribhu K. Kaul
Abstract: The long distance behavior of two-point functions of critical lattice statistical mechanics models depend on detailed information of the underlying quantum field theory. Yet these universal properties can be difficult to extract because generic lattice operators couple to the infinite hierarchy of continuum scaling operators with unknown coefficients. Recently in Physical Review E 114, 024146 (2026), one approach to disentangle some of this information was proposed using the eigen-decomposition of the operator covariance matrix constructed from cross correlations of a finite set of $N_\ell$ lattice operators. In this work we analyze this method in detail. We provide an explicit proof that under the assumption of scale invariance the leading decay of the eigenvalues are controlled by the smallest $N_\ell$ scaling dimensions, even after taking irrelevant perturbations into account. Based on this understanding, we propose a new method by which the eigenvectors of the covariance matrix can be used to construct the amplitudes of the continuum two-point correlation functions. This allows for a complete determination of the structure of the continuum two-point functions from the lattice covariance matrix. We demonstrate our ideas both on synthetic data sets (drawn from the scaling hypothesis) and numerically exact data for the two dimensional Ising model.

### Paper [319]
Title: A Transport-Information Framework for Continuous-Fiber-Aligned Coarse-Graining Representation Sets
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27811
Authors: Koretaka Yuge
Abstract: Consider representation sets of interest whose elements are associated with continuous realizations related to them through coarse-graining (CG). CG can be regarded as an operation that reduces a representation by discarding information that is not retained in its output. However, a systematic description of the information structure associated with CG in general remains to be established. Here, we propose a transport-information framework for continuous-fiber-aligned representation sets, in which each representation is associated with a continuous realization through a fiber structure, with CG defining the projection onto the representation. Within this framework, CG is described through a local-map operation that transports continuous probability mass onto representative points. The resulting transport cost is expressed as the contraction of the second-moment structure of the underlying continuous representation with the transport metric. Building on the previously proposed framework of unavoidable canonical nonlinearity (UCN), this construction provides a non-arbitrary information-theoretic interpretation of the CG-associated transport cost in terms of information indistinguishability, quantified by an expected second-order structure on statistical manifold associated with the CG geometry. We also show that the proposed framework can also be naturally extended to the CG of the discrete measures themselves. The present framework provides a basis for describing information costs associated with individual CG operations and for comparing representation sets while accounting for their underlying continuous realizations.

### Paper [320]
Title: Symmetry-Guided Machine Learning of Spin-Dependent Nonlinear Hall Response
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28529
Authors: Jyotirmoy Sau
Abstract: A Berry-curvature dipole can generate a current perpendicular to an applied electric field even when the linear Hall response is zero. This work examines how lattice symmetry, magnetic orientation, and carrier occupation control this nonlinear response in an anisotropic honeycomb model. Unequal hopping permits one dipole component without magnetism. Rotating the local moments can activate the second component and change the sign and magnitude of both. Reversing all moments leaves the dipole unchanged but reverses the intrinsic linear Hall conductivity. Equal populations of reversed domains can therefore retain the dipole contribution when their local electric fields are the same. A symmetry-constrained polynomial machine-learning model describes the calculated dipoles for 1024 spin configurations. At the reference chemical potential, both components have $R^2>0.9995$ for 256 configurations excluded from training. Keeping the carrier density fixed shifts the chemical potential as the moments rotate and gives slightly larger prediction errors. Separate fits across a chemical-potential sweep show a stronger loss of accuracy for $D_y$ at higher energies. Within weak-field semiclassical transport with a constant relaxation time, the predicted dipole determines the angular dependence, rectification, and second harmonic of the current. The results connect magnetic control of the nonlinear Hall response to the balance of Berry-curvature contributions near the Fermi level.

### Paper [321]
Title: Mechanistic discovery in grain boundary structure and deformation driven by agentic AI
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28629
Authors: Yazhuo Liu, Kunqing Ding, Xiaoguo Gong, Zifeng Wang, Yichen Yang, Yifeng Che, Wen Chen, Josh Kacher, Olivier Pierron, Shigenobu Ogata, Ting Zhu
Abstract: Agentic artificial intelligence (AI) systems hold immense potential for automating complex workflows in materials discovery and design, but extending these capabilities to domain-intensive mechanistic studies remains challenging. General grain boundaries (GBs) exemplify this challenge, as their structural complexity complicates experimental characterization and atomistic modeling. Here, we present GBClaw, an agentic AI framework for investigating the structure and deformation of general GBs, which are common in real polycrystals yet remain understudied. By integrating large language models into a modular architecture, GBClaw effectively combines systematic simulations with targeted mechanistic analysis through iterative workflow refinement and dynamic replanning. Applied to 14 general asymmetric tilt GBs in nickel, the framework characterized interfacial defect structures and deformation modes across these incommensurate boundaries. It established a general approach to identify previously unobserved, widely spaced secondary GB misfit dislocations (SGBMDs) alongside closely spaced primary GB misfit dislocations (PGBMDs) in simulated atomic configurations and experimental images. Comparisons of GB sliding with and without SGBMDs revealed substantial differences in shear strength, underscoring the critical role of misfit-dislocation hierarchies in sliding resistance. Researcher-guided follow-up calculations further elucidated the structural origins of shear asymmetry and an anomalous shear response. This work demonstrates that combining agentic AI with domain expertise bridges high-throughput computational screening and deep mechanistic discovery at complex material interfaces.

### Paper [322]
Title: Geometry-controlled zeros of resonant Kelvin-wave conversion in four spatial dimensions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29218
Authors: Wei-can Yang, Xiang-dong Xu, Cheng Li, Ke-yu Lu
Abstract: We show that geometry can suppress resonant four-wave conversion of Kelvin waves on a vortex surface in four spatial dimensions. Perpendicular incoming waves eliminate the entire local-area contribution, exposing a finite interaction mediated by the surrounding fluid. Reflection allows the incoming wave-number ratio to vary at exact resonance, revealing a sign-changing zero of this fluid coupling at \(\lambda_*\simeq1.469\). Uniform matching of the Gross--Pitaevskii (GP) four-wave coupling to the fluid result establishes microscopic zeros that approach this ratio at long wavelengths. Numerical calculations also show the corresponding sign change. Independent calculations in separate resonant geometries connect the coupling sign to the phase of coherently generated waves and the frequency ordering of reflection-even and reflection-odd modes. The surrounding flow thus determines whether an exactly resonant conversion channel is present at four-wave order.

### Paper [323]
Title: Tuning Thermal Memory and Dissipation in Colloidal Microgel Glasses via Particle Stiffness
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29389
Authors: Sonali Vasant Kawale, Ranjini Bandyopadhyay
Abstract: The Kovacs effect, characterized by nonlinear, path-dependent structural recovery, is a hallmark of glassy systems. In soft colloidal microgel glasses, particle stiffness governs deformation, swelling, rearrangement, and response to external stimuli, and thereby dictates the path-dependent structural recovery of these jammed non-equilibrium systems. To investigate this influence, microgels of different stiffnesses were synthesized by adjusting the concentration of the polar crosslinker, N, N methylenebisacrylamide (MBA). The relaxation of the storage and loss moduli was measured using oscillatory rheology under diverse temperature ramp rates, and cryo-FESEM imaging provided direct visualization of the underlying particle packing. While the rapid adaptability of soft particles and the kinetic arrest of stiff ones suppresses Kovacs asymmetry, intermediate-stiffness particles exhibit moderate thermoresponsivity, traversing distinct energy landscapes on heating and cooling to maximize asymmetry and dissipation. Cryo-FESEM micrographs, displaying gel-like networks in soft particle suspensions, dense packing in stiff particle suspensions, and disordered, compact structures in intermediate-stiffness systems, corroborate these findings. Our results demonstrate that intermediate-stiffness PNIPAM microgels optimize structural recovery, rendering them promising candidates for forming colloidal glasses, and establish Kovacs asymmetry as a robust probe of non-equilibrium dynamics in glassy systems.

### Paper [324]
Title: Sub-monolayer tracking of molecular states at buried interfaces
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31924
Authors: Harlan Heilman, Em Biegler, Sushil Adhikari, Victor Murcia, Tanner Melody, Fred Woodall, Lars Thomsen, Obaid Alqahtani, Brian A. Collins
Abstract: Phenomena responsible for emerging technologies originate from novel molecular states at buried interfaces. Yet quantitatively resolving these features is out of reach in most cases. Resonant soft X-ray scattering (RSoXS) has demonstrated sensitivity to these local states but is limited due to a lack of accurate interpretive models and spatial resolution. Here we demonstrate a machine learning (ML)-optimized optical model that establishes the direct and quantitative interpretation of molecular orientation, chemical identity and packing states with sub-monolayer resolution - reaching beyond the diffraction limit. We discover a gradient evolution of molecular orientation and packing emerging from a pinned state directly at the channel-dielectric interface of an organic transistor, indicative of competing surface versus bulk growth dynamics that contradict models based on substrate patterning. This non-destructive insight into buried interfacial formation and final configuration opens possibilities in controlling interfacial states and phenomena in next-generation technologies.

### Paper [325]
Title: Temperature-dependent magnetic properties of the antiferromagnet Mn$_2$Au
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35153
Authors: Jackson L. Ross, Sarah Jenkins, Ricardo Rama-Eiroa, Paul-Iulian Gavriloaea, Jack Collins, Unai Atxitia, Oksana Chubykalo-Fesenko, Karin Everschor-Sitte, Rub\'en M. Otxoa, Roy W. Chantrell, Richard F. L. Evans
Abstract: Mn$_2$Au is one of the most promising materials for antiferromagnetic spintronics due to its high N\'eel temperature, moderate anisotropy, layered spin structure allowing current induced spin-orbit-torques, and ultrafast spin dynamics. Further understanding and control of the magnetic order in Mn$_2$Au for ultrafast spintronics applications via ultrashort electric/optical means requires accurate understanding of how its magnetic properties depend on temperature. Here we present atomistic spin dynamics simulations of the temperature-dependent macroscopic magnetic properties of Mn$_2$Au single crystals. We introduce a spin Hamiltonian based on previous ab initio calculations which produces temperature-dependent scaling of the anisotropy constants with magnetisation consistent with Zener-Akulov-Callen-Callen power laws. The calculated temperature dependence of exchange stiffness follows a scaling law with magnetisation of $m^{1.83}_e$ , and predicts a temperature-dependent domain wall width that agrees very well with direct simulation. Our model provides an understanding of temperature-dependent magnetic properties of Mn$_2$Au that enables large-scale simulation of the working conditions of spintronic devices.

### Paper [326]
Title: Collinear and Coplanar Altermagnetism in a Penrose Quasicrystal
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35855
Authors: Bai-He Yao, Zhi-Yan Shao, Ye Cao, Fan Yang
Abstract: We generalize the previous group-theory based classification of collinear magnetism in the quasicrystal (QC) to general magnetic states. Taking the Penrose lattice as an example, we find that the magnetic states corresponding to 2D irreducible representations (IRRPs) of the $D_5$ point group represent for a new type of altermagnetism (AM). Unlike conventional AM wherein zero magnetism is protected by, e.g. rotation or mirror reflection, which globally flips the spin in the magnetic pattern, the zero magnetism in these 2D-IRRP states is instead protected by a special group-representation-orthogonality theorem which states that basis functions belonging to different IRRPs are mutually orthogonal. This special compensation mechanism and the absence of $\mathcal{PT}$ symmetry finally lead to AM states. Taking the Penrose-Hubbard model as an example, our linear-response theory and Ginzburg-Landau theory based analysis show that the largest portion of the parameter space is occupied by such 2D-IRRP states, which can be either collinear or coplanar AM states. These special AM states are characterized by spin split in the spin-resolved angular-resolved photoemission spectroscopy and the spin conductance with unique symmetry features. These intriguing collinear and coplanar AM states result from symmetry and can also emerge in other QCs.

### Paper [327]
Title: Same-spin correlation screens the exchange peak of the electron gas near 2kF
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35896
Authors: Carl A. Kukkonen
Abstract: Exchange alone gives the electron gas a local field factor with a sharp peak just below 2kF. Quantum Monte Carlo finds none. Both spin channels are computed here from their lowest diagrams with a dynamically screened interaction and no fitting. Parallel spin correlation is the negative image of exchange and removes about half the peak; the calculated spin-resolved factors account for the QMC data at rs = 1 and 2, and at q = 0 for the compressibility and spin susceptibility obtained from the ground-state energy. The two classes of diagrams are the two terms of the kernel of Hedin's vertex equation, of which the second is routinely dropped.

### Paper [328]
Title: Bayesian Inference in Partially Observed Critical Systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35973
Authors: Mihika Singhania
Abstract: We ask how much of a large random system must be observed before a global average can be inferred with controlled uncertainty. For the random transposition shuffle, a conjugate Bayesian estimator of the average displacement, computed from $m$ uniformly sampled cards, has relative uncertainty $1/\sqrt{2m}$, independent of the deck size $n$. The law of total variance exposes a second error, the floor $R_n$, which arises because the observed configuration is a single draw from equilibrium: no relative tolerance below $\kappa\sqrt{R_n}$ can be certified, even by a census. For the shuffle we derive $R_n=(2n^2+7)/\bigl(5(n-1)^2(n+1)\bigr)$ and show that the posterior remains calibrated. At the critical point of the two-dimensional Ising model the floor of the energy vanishes, whereas that of the magnetisation stays near $0.072$; its mean-field limit is $\sqrt{2}-1$. For correlated fields read by sensor networks, the usual $95\%$ interval covers the ensemble mean in $7\%$ of cases. Bounding the floor from one realisation, as in single-chain Monte Carlo diagnostics, requires an assumption on the decay of correlations.

### Paper [329]
Title: Stability reversal, activated escape, and mechanical response of negative-mobility particles
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36884
Authors: Junteng Li, Zhihong You
Abstract: Understanding the fluctuations and response of particles in potential landscapes is a fundamental problem in nonequilibrium statistical physics. Here, we investigate how negative mobility reshapes the stochastic behavior of a particle moving in a mechanical potential landscape. We consider a one-dimensional overdamped particle subject to additive noise, with mobility that is negative at weak forces and positive at strong forces. In a potential well, negative mobility destabilizes the mechanical potential minimum and produces bimodal localization at two off-center positions. The same stability reversal transforms conventional Kramers escape from a potential well into a two-stage escape process involving an intermediate metastable state. When an additional spatially uniform force is applied, the mechanical perturbation translates rather than tilts the effective double well, yielding a positive response dominated at weak noise by intrawell relaxation, even though slow interwell switching dominates spontaneous positional fluctuations. These findings reveal how negative mobility reshapes the interplay among mechanical landscapes, stochastic stability, and response.

### Paper [330]
Title: Strain Functionals: A Complete and Symmetry-adapted Set of Descriptors to Characterize Atomistic Configurations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.04191
Authors: Edward M. Kober, Jacob P. Tavenner, Colin M. Adams, Nithin Mathew
Abstract: Extracting relevant information from atomistic simulations relies on a complete and accurate characterization of atomistic configurations. We present a framework for characterizing atomistic configurations in terms of a complete and symmetry-adapted basis, referred to as strain functionals. In this approach a Gaussian kernel is used to map discrete atomic quantities, such as number density, velocities, and forces, to continuous fields. The local atomic configurations are then characterized using nth order central moments of the local number density. The initial Cartesian moments are recast unitarily into a Solid Harmonic Polynomial basis using SO(3) decompositions. Rotationally invariant metrics, referred to as Strain Functional Descriptors (SFDs), are constructed from the terms in the SO(3) decomposition using Clebsch-Gordan coupling. A key distinction compared to related methods is that a minimal but complete set of descriptors is identified. These descriptors characterize the local geometries numerically in terms of shape, density, and orientation descriptors that recognize n-fold symmetry axes and net shapes such as trigonal, cubic, hexagonal, etc. They can easily distinguish between most different crystal symmetries using n = 4, identify defects (such as dislocations and stacking faults), measure local deformation, and can be used in conjunction with machine learning techniques for in situ analysis of finite temperature atomistic simulation data and quantification of defect dynamics.

### Paper [331]
Title: Dissipative Dynamical Phase Transition as a Complex Ising Model
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.09591
Authors: Stephen W. Yan, Diego Barberena, Matthew P. A. Fisher, Sagar Vijay
Abstract: We investigate a quantum dynamical phase transition induced by the competition between local unitary evolution and dissipation in a qubit chain with a strong, on-site $\mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetry. While the steady-state of this evolution is always maximally-mixed, we show that the dynamical behavior of certain non-local observables on the approach to this steady-state is dictated by a quantum Ising model with a complex transverse-field (cTFIM). A crucial feature of our mapping to the cTFIM is an extensive number of local, weak, $\mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetries possessed by the dynamics, allowing us to investigate these observables analytically and uncover a dynamical phase transition as the relative rate of unitary evolution and dissipation is tuned. We show that the weak-dissipation limit corresponds to a cTFIM with a large magnitude of the imaginary transverse-field, for which the many-body "ground-state" (with smallest real eigenvalue) is gapless, exhibiting quasi-long-range correlations of the local magnetization with a continuously-varying exponent. Correspondingly, the dynamics of the non-local observables show oscillatory behavior with an amplitude decaying exponentially in time. The strong-dissipation limit corresponds to a gapped ferromagnetic phase of the cTFIM, and non-local observables show exponential decay on the approach to equilibrium. This transition in (1+1)-dimensions has a peculiar, "two-sided" nature appearing as either first- or second-order depending on the phase from which the transition is approached, an analytic result which is corroborated by numerical studies. In higher dimensions, we present a field-theoretic understanding of the first-order nature of this transition, when approaching from the ferromagnetic phase of the cTFIM, though the nature of the phase with large imaginary transverse-field remains to be understood.

### Paper [332]
Title: Classification of locality preserving symmetries on spin chains
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15088
Authors: Alex Bols, Wojciech De Roeck, Michiel De Wilde, Bruno de O. Carvalho
Abstract: We consider the action of a finite group $G$ by locality preserving automorphisms (quantum cellular automata) on quantum spin chains. We refer to such group actions as ``symmetries''. The natural notion of equivalence for such symmetries is \emph{stable equivalence}, which allows for stacking with factorized group actions. Stacking also endows the set of equivalence classes with a group structure. We prove that the anomaly of such symmetries provides an isomorphism between the group of stable equivalence classes of symmetries with the cohomology group $H^3(G,U(1))$, consistent with previous conjectures. This amounts to a complete classification of locality preserving symmetries on spin chains.

### Paper [333]
Title: Four-Fermion Deformations on Line Defects in QED$_4$ with Yukawas
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.19686
Authors: Alessandra D'Alise, Giulia Muco, Francesco Sannino
Abstract: We elucidate the dynamics induced by a Wilson Line of charge $q$ and electron charge $e$ in $QED_4$ coupled to a real scalar via Yukawa interactions. We observe that there is a range of the fixed product $e^2 q$ with $e\rightarrow 0$ and $q\rightarrow \infty$ for which four-fermion operators play a dominant role for the dynamics of the theory. We uncover the fixed-point dynamics of the theory and analyze their critical behavior.

### Paper [334]
Title: Tight Generalization of Robertson-Type Uncertainty Relations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19861
Authors: Gen Kimura, Aina Mayumi, Haruki Yamashita
Abstract: We establish the tightest possible Robertson-type preparation uncertainty relation, which explicitly depends on the eigenvalues of the quantum state. The conventional constant $ \tfrac{1}{4} $ is replaced by the state-dependent coefficient \[ \frac{(\lambda_{\max} + \lambda_{\min})^2}{4(\lambda_{\max} - \lambda_{\min})^2}, \] where $ \lambda_{\max} $ and $\lambda_{\min}$ denote the largest and smallest eigenvalues of the density operator $ \rho $, respectively. We prove that this coefficient is optimal among all Robertson-type generalizations and does not admit further improvement. We also obtain a complete characterization of all nontrivial equality cases. Our relation becomes more pronounced as the quantum state becomes more mixed, capturing a trade-off in quantum uncertainty that the conventional Robertson's relation fails to detect. In addition, our result also provides a strict generalization of the Schr\"odinger's uncertainty relation, showing that the uncertainty trade-off is governed by the sum of the covariance term and a state-dependent improvement over the Robertson bound. As applications, we also refine error-disturbance trade-offs by incorporating spectral information of both the system and the measuring apparatus, thereby generalizing the Arthurs-Goodman and Ozawa inequalities.

### Paper [335]
Title: Entanglement Scaling of Motzkin Chains under Spiral Hamiltonian-path Embeddings
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02103
Authors: Zhao Zhang, Olai B. Mykland
Abstract: We investigate how a two-dimensional spatial embedding changes the entanglement observed across geometrically local bipartitions of a one-dimensional quantum spin chain. A $q$-deformed colored Motzkin chain is placed along a spiral-inspired Hamiltonian path that visits $N(L)=\Theta(L^2)$ sites of a square-lattice region while retaining a path interaction graph. Straight ambient-space cuts then correspond to unions of $\Theta(L)$ disconnected intervals in the chain ordering. We derive the scaling of a height-variation proxy for the leading color-pairing contribution and predict ambient-space entropy scaling proportional to $L^2$, $L$, and $O(1)$ for $q>1$, $q=1$, and $q<1$, respectively. We evaluate this proxy numerically using the matrix-product-state representation and verify its predicted finite-size scaling. The embedded and original Hamiltonians have exactly identical spectra and spectral gaps at fixed chain length. We also describe a sparse tensor-network representation and propose a two-chain junction extension. These results isolate the roles of interaction connectivity, spatial embedding, and subsystem geometry in entanglement scaling.

### Paper [336]
Title: Decoding cell signaling via optimal transport and information theory
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18028
Authors: Mintu Nandi, Sosuke Ito
Abstract: Cellular signal processing performs reliably despite molecular noise. Mutual information (MI) is widely used to quantify signaling fidelity, capturing how well outputs discriminate input states. However, it fails to capture whether the output preserves the statistical structure of the input, a property crucial in morphogen patterning and dose-dependent signaling. To address this gap, we introduce the 2-Wasserstein (2-WD) distance, which provides a geometric basis for comparing input and output distributions. We define MI as informational fidelity (INF) and the inverse of the 2-WD as geometric fidelity (GMF). Applying this dual-fidelity framework to canonical regulatory motifs under Gaussian channel approximation reveals topology-dependent trade-offs: coherent feed-forward loops can perform well in both dimensions, whereas feedback architectures reduce INF to enhance GMF. Experimental analysis of tumor necrosis factor signaling reveals dual-fidelity behavior qualitatively consistent with feedback regulation. RAS-MAPK data analysis further shows that jointly considering INF and GMF better characterizes intracellular signal relay than INF alone. Our results thus indicate that these signaling behaviors are not fully characterized by MI alone; instead, distributional correspondence provides a complementary dimension of signaling fidelity. Our study provides a practical framework for analyzing natural networks and guiding the design of task-specific synthetic circuits.

### Paper [337]
Title: Some effective operators for graphene monolayer superlattices, from variational perturbation theory
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.19185
Authors: Louis Garrigue
Abstract: Our goal is to provide precise effective operators for monolayer graphene at Fermi energy. We consider the microscopic potential created by a lattice, and add a macroscopic potential with the same periodicity but varying at a scale $\varepsilon^{-1} \in \mathbb{N}$, creating a superlattice. Our approach combines a variational approximation with perturbation theory and a multiscale method. At the effective level the usual massless Dirac operator is enriched by other operators. We provide simulations for graphene to show the increased accuracy, and a theoretical result proving the improved validity time scale in the dynamical case.

### Paper [338]
Title: Partial Reversibility and Counterdiabatic Driving in Nearly Integrable Systems
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22317
Authors: Rohan Banerjee, Shahyad Khamnei, Anatoli Polkovnikov, Stewart Morawetz
Abstract: Adiabatic (or reversible) processes are the key concept unifying our understanding of thermodynamics and dynamical systems. Reversibility in the thermodynamic sense is understood as entropy-preserving processes, such as in the idealized Carnot engine, whereas in integrable dynamical systems it is understood as the conservation of the action variables. Between these two idealized limits, however, things are much less clear. In this work, we study several toy models to assess the extent to which reversible processes are even possible in this regime. We then explore how the dissipative losses resulting from rapidly driving these kinds of systems can be fought by approximate counterdiabatic driving. Finally, we provide numerical evidence that much of the phenomenology should be the same for quantum many-body systems with large degeneracy in the presence of integrability breaking perturbations.

### Paper [339]
Title: Extreme Quantum Cognition Machines for Deliberative Decision Making
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05430
Authors: Francesco Romeo, Jacopo Settino
Abstract: We introduce Extreme Quantum Cognition Machines, a class of quantum learning architectures for deliberative decision making designed to operate under coarse-grained, ambiguous, and potentially contradictory information. Inspired by the quantum cognition paradigm, Extreme Quantum Cognition Machines are closely related to quantum extreme learning and quantum reservoir computing, where fixed quantum dynamics generates a nonlinear feature map and learning is confined to a linear readout. A dynamical attention mechanism, implemented through an input-dependent interaction term in the Hamiltonian, modulates the quantum evolution and biases the resulting feature embedding toward task-relevant correlations. The approach is validated on linguistic classification tasks, which serve as paradigmatic examples of deliberative inference. Hardware-compatible quantum implementations of the proposed framework are discussed, together with potential applications in symbolic inference, sequence analysis, anomaly detection, and automatic diagnosis, with direct relevance to domains such as biology, forensics, and cybersecurity.

### Paper [340]
Title: Universal purification dynamics in real non-unitary quantum processes
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10751
Authors: Federico Gerbino, Donghoon Kim, Guido Giachetti, Andrea De Luca, Xhek Turkeshi
Abstract: We study purification dynamics in monitored quantum processes governed by ensembles of quantum circuits in different random-matrix symmetry classes. We analyze the universal aspects that emerge away from the measurement induced phase transition and inside the volume/weak measurement phase and in the scaling limit of large time and Hilbert space dimension. We present two toy models that reveal two complementary visions and provide quantitative access to universal scaling: i) a discrete-time dynamic in which each time step corresponds to multiplication by a Gaussian random matrix; ii) weak continuous-time monitoring that induces a Dyson brownian motion of the eigenvalues of the density matrix. The first approach provides an algebraic characterization based on rotational invariance emerging in Kraus's operator space, focusing in particular on the unitary and orthogonal cases, respectively $\beta=2$ and $\beta=1$, with $\beta$ the Dyson random-matrix index. The second approach, on the other hand, allows for a unified treatment for any $\beta$, thanks to the mapping of the Fokker-Planck evolution of eigenvalues onto the Calogero-Sutherland integrable Hamiltonian diagonalized in terms of Jack polynomials. We provide explicit expressions for the universal decrease of R\'enyi entropies. We show that, approaching the universal scaling limit, numerical simulations of different models agree with each other and with our theoretical predictions. Our results clarify the existence of different classes of universality for the purification process in hybrid quantum systems, accessible in random circuit architectures and weak measurement protocols.

### Paper [341]
Title: Bootstrapping Fermionic $R$-operators with Decorated Yang--Baxter Equations
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11172
Authors: Zhao Zhang
Abstract: Integrating infinitesimal generators of local dynamics into finite-time evolution is generally harder than extracting a local Hamiltonian from a quantum gate. For integrable one-dimensional quantum many-body systems, a recent bootstrap constructs one-parameter $R$-operators directly from Hamiltonians satisfying the Reshetikhin condition. However, this condition excludes important models such as the Hubbard model, whose two-parameter $R$-operator was obtained by Shastry from a decorated Yang--Baxter equation obeyed by its free-fermion sector. Here, I extend the bootstrap to $R$-operators constrained by both the ordinary and decorated Yang--Baxter equations. Their joint expansion produces additional Hamiltonian-level necessary conditions and recursively determines candidate higher-order terms, subject to two-site locality at each order. I also derive compatibility and factorization conditions under which a Shastry-type superposition solves the $RLL$ relation after deformation by an onsite involution. The construction recovers the known two-parameter $R$-operators of the Hubbard and XYh models, whose full Yang--Baxter equations follow from established tetrahedral Zamolodchikov algebra relations. For a superconducting Hubbard deformation, the scalar equations are generically incompatible, placing it outside this framework. These results provide diagnostics and a systematic route to fermionic $R$-operators beyond the Reshetikhin condition. General sufficiency, convergence of the formal bootstrap, and sufficiency of the deformation conditions remain open.

### Paper [342]
Title: Bridging the Simulation-to-Experiment Gap with Generative Models using Adversarial Distribution Alignment
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01169
Authors: Kai Nelson, Tobias Kreiman, Sergey Levine, Aditi S. Krishnapriyan
Abstract: A fundamental challenge in science and engineering is the simulation-to-experiment gap. While we often possess prior knowledge of physical laws, these physical laws can be too difficult to solve exactly for complex systems. Such systems are commonly modeled using simulators, which impose computational approximations. Meanwhile, experimental measurements more faithfully represent the real world, but experimental data typically consists of observations that only partially reflect the system's full underlying state. We propose a data-driven distribution alignment framework that bridges this simulation-to-experiment gap by pre-training a generative model on fully observed (but imperfect) simulation data, then aligning it with partial (but real) observations of experimental data. While our method is domain-agnostic, we ground our approach in the physical sciences by introducing Adversarial Distribution Alignment (ADA). This method aligns a generative model of atomic positions -- initially trained on a simulated Boltzmann distribution -- with the distribution of experimental observations. We prove that our method recovers the target observable distribution, even with multiple, potentially correlated observables. We also empirically validate our framework on synthetic, molecular, and experimental protein data, demonstrating that it can align generative models with diverse observables. Our code is available at https://kaityrusnelson.com/ada/.

### Paper [343]
Title: Fast projections of two-dimensional light patterns using acousto-optical deflectors
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19421
Authors: Robbert Decruyenaere, Clara Tanghe, Senne Van Wellen, Karel Van Acoleyen
Abstract: Precise and flexible control of structured light fields is essential for applications ranging from optical trapping and quantum simulation to microscopy and materials processing. Acousto-optical deflectors (AODs) are widely used in these settings due to their high speed, large damage threshold, and ability to generate steerable optical tweezers. Multi-tone driving offers a powerful alternative to slow sequential scanning, enabling the projection of complex patterns with high accuracy as rapid acoustic modulation averages out inter-spot interference. In two dimensions, however, intermodulation between tones in orthogonal AODs can reintroduce coherent artifacts. We present a fast, feedback-free AOD projection scheme based on an incommensurately staggered frequency lattice that intrinsically suppresses such artifacts. For separable two-dimensional target patterns, our method removes the need for scanning entirely, enabling substantially faster and highly accurate projections. We further extend the approach to non-separable images using a minimal scanning strategy that maintains rather high projection speeds. These results demonstrate that appropriately engineered multi-tone AOD driving offers an efficient and robust route to high-speed, high-fidelity generation of arbitrary intensity patterns.

### Paper [344]
Title: Algorithmic Locality of Quantum Tensor Network Belief Propagation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21919
Authors: Siddhant Midha, Yifan F. Zhang, Daniel Malz, Dmitry A. Abanin, Sarang Gopalakrishnan
Abstract: Belief propagation has recently emerged as a powerful framework for evaluating tensor networks in higher dimensions, combining computational efficiency with provable analytical guarantees. In this work, we develop the first end-to-end theory of tensor network belief propagation for a class of projected entangled pair states satisfying \emph{strong injectivity}. We show that when the injectivity parameter exceeds a constant threshold, BP fixed points can be found efficiently, and a cluster-corrected BP algorithm computes physical quantities to $1/\mathrm{poly}(N)$ error in $\mathrm{poly}(N)$ time for an $N$ qubit system. We further identify a striking phenomenon we term \emph{algorithmic locality}: local perturbations of the tensor network affect the BP fixed point with an influence decaying rapidly with distance. Moreover, through the cluster expansion, this locality extends to observables, implying that local expectation values can be approximated from local data with controlled accuracy. We exhibit three regimes admitting strong injectivity: random Gaussian PEPS with large physical dimension, high-temperature Gibbs states of commuting two-body Hamiltonians, and fixed-depth quantum circuits subject to sufficiently strong depolarizing noise. Our bounds are worst-case sufficient conditions and do not identify sharp algorithmic transitions.

### Paper [345]
Title: Remotely programming the weights of a spintronic neural network by a radiofrequency broadcast signal
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24561
Authors: M. Menshawy (Laboratoire Albert Fert, CNRS, Thales, Universit\'e Paris-Saclay, Palaiseau, France), D. Sanz-Hern\'andez (Laboratoire Albert Fert, CNRS, Thales, Universit\'e Paris-Saclay, Palaiseau, France), L. Mazza (Department of Electrical and Information Engineering, Politecnico di Bari, Bari, Italy), V. Puliafito (Department of Electrical and Information Engineering, Politecnico di Bari, Bari, Italy), G. Finocchio (Department of Mathematical and Computer Sciences, Physical Sciences and Earth Sciences, University of Messina, Messina, Italy), A. Jenkins (International Iberian Nanotechnology Laboratory, Braga, Portugal), R. Ferreira (International Iberian Nanotechnology Laboratory, Braga, Portugal), L. Benetti (International Iberian Nanotechnology Laboratory, Braga, Portugal), J. Grollier (Laboratoire Albert Fert, CNRS, Thales, Universit\'e Paris-Saclay, Palaiseau, France), F. A. Mizrahi (Laboratoire Albert Fert, CNRS, Thales, Universit\'e Paris-Saclay, Palaiseau, France)
Abstract: Selectively programming a large number of non-volatile synaptic weights without compromising scalability is a key challenge for in-memory computing. Here, we experimentally demonstrate remote programming of synaptic weights in series-connected chains of 11 vortex-based magnetic tunnel junctions using broadcast radiofrequency signals applied through a shared strip line. Because each junction is engineered with a distinct resonance frequency, programming relies on frequency-selective reversal of the vortex-core polarity and therefore does not require individual access lines or selector devices. Reconfiguring the binary states of these chains strongly reshapes the continuous spectral transfer functions that determine the weighted sums performed on frequency-multiplexed RF inputs. This allows compact networks built from such chains to process high-dimensional RF-encoded inputs. We experimentally perform handwritten-digit classification and drone RF-signature identification using a two-chain network comprising only 22 synapses in total. After remote reconfiguration for each task, the spintronic network respectively reaches 93.9 +/- 2.3 % and 99.5 +/- 1 % test accuracy, on par with the software baseline. Broadcast RF programming thus provides a compact and scalable route to rapidly reconfigurable spintronic neuromorphic hardware.

### Paper [346]
Title: Pole Skipping, Avoided Crossing, and Resonant Excitation in Kerr Quasinormal Modes near Algebraically Special Frequencies
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17840
Authors: Kei-ichiro Kubota, Hayato Motohashi
Abstract: Kerr quasinormal modes near algebraically special frequencies exhibit anomalous behavior, including apparent bifurcation, disappearance, and a nonsmooth connection to the Schwarzschild limit, which has remained puzzling for decades. Tracking poles and zeros of Green-function building blocks across different Riemann sheets, we show that the bifurcation is due to an avoided crossing accompanied by resonant excitation, while the disappearance is due to pole skipping caused by cancellation of a quasinormal-mode pole by a Matsubara-mode zero. This resolves the physical origin of these long-standing anomalies.

### Paper [347]
Title: The bulk spectral gap is certifiable from above but uncomputable from below
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03836
Authors: Xiangling Xu, Matthias Sch\"otz, Jie Wang, Victor Magron, Igor Klep, Omar Fawzi, Marc-Olivier Renou
Abstract: Determining spectral gaps in the thermodynamic limit is a central challenge in quantum many-body physics. When a fixed boundary condition is imposed, this problem is undecidable: no algorithm, even with unlimited resources, can decide for every Hamiltonian whether it is gapped. Yet the thermodynamic limit is meant to capture the intrinsic properties of very large systems, which should not depend on how their boundaries are chosen. We therefore consider the bulk spectral gap, which concerns only the intrinsic excitations of the infinite system, and ask what can and cannot be computed about it. First, we give a general algorithm producing a hierarchy of certified upper bounds on the bulk gap, arbitrarily tight at the cost of more computation. Second, we prove that the spectral gap problem remains undecidable in the bulk, already for translationally invariant nearest-neighbour chains of fixed local dimension. Hence no general algorithm produces convergent lower bounds, and no convergent upper-bounding algorithm, ours included, admits a computable convergence rate. The bulk spectral gap is thus certifiable from above but uncomputable from below; more precisely, the bulk-gaplessness promise problem is RE-complete, exactly as hard as the halting problem. The same construction shows that no general algorithm can decide whether a given product state is a ground state, and that convergent bounds on ground-state expectation values admit no computable convergence rate in general. Finally, we obtain the first nontrivial certified upper bounds on the bulk gap of the spin-1/2 kagome Heisenberg antiferromagnet.

### Paper [348]
Title: Prospective Coding and Path Integration Emerge as Equilibrium Solutions of Self-Organizing Neural Networks with Firing-Rate Adaptation
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14649
Authors: Facundo Emina, Emilio Kropff
Abstract: Continuous Attractor Neural Networks (CANNs) traditionally rely on pre-wired recurrent connectivity to model spatial representations, path integration, and anticipatory dynamics. However, the biological mechanisms through which this structured connectivity emerges via learning remain relatively unexplored. This work presents a theoretical framework revealing how continuous attractor connectivity and its computational properties self-organize through Hebbian plasticity, firing-rate adaptation, and global inhibition. We show that translationally invariant inputs naturally drive the emergence of stable, Gaussian-profiled feedforward weights. Crucially, anticipatory dynamics arise spontaneously within these feedforward architectures, shifting the activity bump forward without requiring recurrent excitatory collaterals. This predictive shift can be linearly amplified across multilayer networks, consistent with anticipatory activity observed in the superficial layers of the entorhinal cortex. Furthermore, introducing recurrent interactions allows the network to learn connections capable of self-sustaining a moving bump of activity. Finally, by modulating the network with an external, time-varying baseline current that encodes speed, the system adjusts its intrinsic velocity to function as a precise unidirectional path integrator. Ultimately, this study suggests that prospective coding and path integration are not manually engineered features, but rather naturally co-emergent properties of a single self-organizing competitive network.

### Paper [349]
Title: Superselection Rigidity of Topological Calderbank--Shor--Steane Codes: From Koszul-Complex Models to Nonsplit Extensions
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09913
Authors: Hao Song
Abstract: Can excitation structure classify topological quantum codes up to finite-depth local circuits? For translation-invariant topological Calderbank--Shor--Steane (CSS) codes in arbitrary dimensions, we introduce excitation superselection profiles $\mathscr E_\sigma$ ($\sigma=X,Z$). Their cohomology layers $E_\sigma^\ell:=H^\ell(\mathscr E_\sigma)$ encode excitation types, fusion rules, and translation action, while $\mathscr E_\sigma$ retains cross-degree gluing. Pointlike, looplike, and higher-dimensional excitations correspond to degrees $\ell=1,2,\ldots$, respectively. Our matrix-level Schanuel-lemma method proves single-degree superselection rigidity: if $\mathscr E_\sigma$ is supported in a single degree $\ell$, then $E_\sigma^\ell$ and $\ell$ fix the stable translation-invariant Clifford class under a finite-resolution hypothesis automatic for prime qudits. If $E_\sigma^\ell$ is also finite, prime-qudit codes decouple into toric-code stacks by Clifford circuits after coarse graining, with the exact minimal threshold set by the translation kernel. We develop a unified topological theory of Koszul-complex models (encompassing toric codes, bivariate-bicycle codes, and Haah's cubic code): for regular models, we compute $E_\sigma^\ell$, prove $\mathscr E_\sigma$ is single-degree, express finite-torus logical modules as $\mathrm{Tor}$ groups, and obtain coarse-grained toric-stack normal forms at full length. Two constructions extend this framework to realize any admissible single-degree profile and build multi-degree extensions. The latter yields eight 3D qubit toric-code extensions with identical layers in both Pauli sectors and trivial translation action, yet distinct size-dependent ground-state degeneracies. The seven nonsplit models require blocking to decouple. Thus, cross-degree gluing carries translation-symmetry-enriched topological information invisible to individual layers.

### Paper [350]
Title: Explosive Synchronization in Inertial Kuramoto Models: Determining Resilience in Power Grids
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00910
Authors: Cook Hyun Kim, Jihye Kim, Sangjoon Park, J\"urgen Kurths, B. Kahng
Abstract: Maintaining power-grid stability is essential for a sustainable energy supply. Using empirical power-grid data, we show that the inertial Kuramoto model with correlated powers and coupling strengths provides a relevant framework for analyzing explosive synchronization and grid stability. We identify two distinct synchronization pathways, initiated by population-driven or coupling-driven seed rotors, that yield different order-parameter behaviors. Inertia, in turn, determines whether the resulting seed clusters retain distinct angular velocities. Similar mechanisms govern recovery from external disturbances, linking power-grid resilience to these two nucleation pathways. Our findings provide a microscopic framework for understanding synchronization and recovery, with implications for stable power-grid operation.

### Paper [351]
Title: Online Surrogate Repair: Decoupling High-Fidelity Feedback from Search Length in Closed-Loop Discovery
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07655
Authors: Xiaotang Feng, Philip Torr, Bruno Andreis
Abstract: Closed-loop AI scientists can generate candidate designs at low marginal computational cost, whereas reliable feedback may require wet-lab synthesis, characterization, or high-fidelity computation. Addressing this imbalance through custom laboratory automation remains infrastructure-intensive and costly, while replacing new experiments with a fixed surrogate leaves persistent model errors that can be amplified by optimization. We propose \emph{online surrogate repair} (OSR), a closed-loop algorithm that uses sparse high-fidelity evaluations to update the surrogate throughout a longer agent search conducted primarily with inexpensive surrogate feedback. An acquisition rule selects which designs from the agent's accumulated proposals receive high-fidelity evaluation, and the resulting labels update the surrogate used in subsequent episodes. Across controlled synthetic environments, we demonstrate that improving global surrogate fit does not necessarily reduce maximum regret, whereas Q90-UCB and expected improvement (EI) substantially reduce regret by directing evaluations toward regions that determine the optimizer's decisions. \osradded{Across LLM and Chemeleon workflows, Online EI reduces the amount of oracle queries required by 84.3--90.3\% for matching discovery counts and by 77.9--81.6\% for matching the quality of the best material found.} Online surrogate repair introduces a third feedback regime between fixed-surrogate operation and high-fidelity feedback after every episode, separating the frequency of high-fidelity evaluation from the duration of the agent's search.

### Paper [352]
Title: Signature of Short-Range Order in Static Response of the Three-Dimensional Electron Gas
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10006
Authors: Muhammed H. G\"une\c{s}, Yubo Yang, Vitaly Gorelov, Miguel A. Morales, Matteo Gatti, Lucia Reining, Shiwei Zhang
Abstract: The three-dimensional electron gas is a fundamental model in condensed matter physics and quantum chemistry, and the exchange-correlation energy derived from it serves as the starting point of \textit{ab initio} computations of materials. However, the wave-vector-dependent response, and hence the static local field factor $G(q)$, has remained without accurate ground-state benchmark in the strongly coupled regime for three decades. Using diffusion Monte Carlo, we calculate the static density-density response function and $G(q)$ across the liquid phase and find a pronounced structure in $G(q)$ at intermediate wave vectors, already visible at metallic densities and growing with increasing interaction strength. We identify it as a fingerprint of short-range order by showing that it is required to reproduce the static structure factor. Our parametrization, valid in the entire liquid phase, predicts a low-energy resonant mode inside the particle-hole continuum.

### Paper [353]
Title: Wald entropy and Wen-Zee type gauge-curvature coupling in 2+1-dimensional gravity
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04476
Authors: Aryan Bhatia, Giandomenico Palumbo, Suraj S. Hegde
Abstract: The Wen-Zee term is a mixed gauge-curvature coupling in the topological Chern-Simons description of a quantum Hall fluid, encoding the electric charge bound to spatial curvature. We investigate a Lorentz-covariant generalization of this coupling, expanding its scope to black-hole spacetimes and determine its contribution to horizon entropy. Such a coupling arises naturally in three-dimensional Chern-Simons gravity based on the Maxwell algebra. We show that its Wald entropy contribution is a horizon boost charge, given by a loop integral of the additional gauge field projected along the tangent to the bifurcation circle. This contribution can be nonzero even when the mixed term vanishes on shell. We show that the integral admits an Abelian holonomy interpretation when the projection direction is covariantly constant. We evaluate the charge on a flat cosmological solution and, using smooth horizon fields and a specified thermal ensemble, on an AdS-Lorentz black hole. These results connect the geometric response familiar from quantum Hall physics to gravitational horizon thermodynamics.

### Paper [354]
Title: Redundant Records of the Past: Unifying Quantum Darwinism and Decoherent Histories
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09845
Authors: Philipp Strasberg
Abstract: There exist two different paradigms for records in isolated quantum systems: decoherent histories (which determines which past events leave formal records) and quantum Darwinism (which determines which current events get redundantly recorded). Neither one implies the other, and connections between the two are scarcely investigated. We unify quantum Darwinism and decoherent histories based on the insight that only redundant records about past events matter. The resulting framework of past redundancy encompasses quantum Darwinism when applied to events at the current time, and it implies the probability sum rule or decoherence of histories. The Bhattacharyya coefficient (or fidelity) plays an important role to establish this connection. Importantly, various examples show that past redundancy is a more widespread phenomenon than the fine-tuned models necessary for quantum Darwinism suggest. We thus arrive at a richer picture in which redundancy can be caused by mechanisms different from quantum Darwinism (such as macroevents or cascade structures), which emphasizes that redundancy is necessary but not sufficient for objectivity, and which, overall, allows to realistically investigate the question: how generic is redundancy in nature?

### Paper [355]
Title: An Instability of the Quantum Markov Length
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12929
Authors: David C. Newsom, Michael Levin
Abstract: The Markov length, the length scale governing the exponential decay of conditional mutual information (CMI) in a quantum many-body state, has proven useful for characterizing mixed-state phases of matter. Nevertheless, we show that a state's Markov length is not generally stable under weak local channels. The failure of stability occurs already at a pure product state. We demonstrate this explicitly in a one-dimensional fermionic chain, where a fine-tuned depth-two circuit of nearest-neighbor Gaussian channels maps the vacuum to a state whose CMI decays \emph{algebraically} and whose Markov length is therefore infinite, even when the channels are taken arbitrarily close to the identity. The algebraic decay is rooted in the nonanalyticity of the von Neumann entropy at rank-deficient density operators. We exhibit an analogous instability on an ordinary qubit chain. More generally, we show that the Markov length is unstable in any translation-invariant state with even a single rank-deficient reduced density operator. These results pose a challenge to recent definitions of mixed-state phase equivalence based on the Markov length.

### Paper [356]
Title: Difference between pair creation and anomaly matching: Nontrivial aspects of invertible symmetries and defects in $SU(N)$ models
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21478
Authors: Yoshiki Fukusumi, Shuma Nakashiba
Abstract: We study the connection between the $SU(N)_{K}$ Wess-Zumino-Witten (WZW) model and $SU(N)_{K'}$ WZW model, enforcing the $\mathbb{Z}_{N}$ symmetries. The fundamental underlying structures are the (half-)integer spin simple currents in the topological quantum field theories and anomaly matching conditions in conformal field theories. For the (half-)integer spin simple current condition, we obtain the generalized pairing condition $K= \pm R^{2}K' (\text{mod}.N)$ with $\text{gcd}(R,N)=1$. On the other hand, the anomaly matching condition results in the weaker condition $K= \pm RUK' (\text{mod}.N)$ with $\text{gcd}(R,N)=\text{gcd}(U,N)=1$. The preservation law of the $\mathbb{Z}_{N}$ defects and symmetries is compatible only with the pairing condition.

### Paper [357]
Title: Physics-Inspired Optimizers beyond the Edge of Stability: Period-Two Orbits and Delayed Instability
URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27321
Authors: Alexander Itin
Abstract: Gradient descent with momentum can be viewed as a particle moving with friction in the loss landscape. In deep learning the learning rate is usually large, so training is a discrete dynamical system: in many full-batch experiments the sharpness grows to the optimizer's stability limit, the edge of stability (EoS), and training continues there. Physics-inspired optimizers change this motion; Velocity-Regularized Adam (VRAdam), for example, shrinks the step at high speed. We study kinetic maps, in which the step is the gradient of a kinetic energy of the momentum, and ask what they do beyond the EoS. (i) In a reduced model, a kinetic map creates a symmetric period-two orbit beyond the edge, strictly linearly stable if and only if the map's elasticity (the log-log slope of step length versus momentum) lies between 0 and 1; on it, training sits at an effective edge of stability. VRAdam's damper fails above twice the critical learning rate, where its kinetic energy is not convex; the Legendre dual of VRAdam's own Lagrangian is convex and passes at every learning rate. (ii) Because the sharpness rises gradually, the loss of stability is delayed, as in Neishtadt's theory of slowly swept bifurcations. In a network with a controlled sweep, the dynamics match this theory without fitted parameters, including the memory of where the sweep started and its erasure by noise. (iii) With momentum, reaching the oscillation also requires elasticity at large momentum: maps that bound the step have competing period-four and chaotic attractors already at beta1 = 0.9, while the cubic-Legendre map reached the oscillation from every sampled start up to beta1 = 0.99. Experiments on an exact quadratic problem, a full-batch MLP and ResNet-32 support the theory; on ResNet-18, much of the apparent benefit of the maps is reproduced by matching the effective learning rate.
